American Journal of Archaeology

Vol. 130, No. 4 · 2026-10-01 · 19 篇 · U Chicago Press (AIA) · Mediterranean / Old World · Anonymized manuscripts + 2 external referees
  1. An Unknown Roman Temple in Cyprus? Reading the Architectural Fragments from Latsithkia (District Limassol, Cyprus)
    Anna Urszula Kordas, Szymon Popławski, Monika Rekowska, Aleksandra Brzozowska-Jawornicka · pp. 483-498
    摘要

    Although Classical Greek religious traditions were well established in Cyprus, Greek-style temples appear to have been relatively rare. Newly documented and analyzed architectural fragments from the Latsithkia area, preserved in the museums of Episkopi and Limassol, suggest that a previously unknown temple may have existed in the chora of Kourion (Cyprus). The aims of this article are to present the architectural fragments with their stylistic and structural analysis and to explore the potential identification and location of the building from which they may have originated, while emphasizing the tentative nature of the reconstruction and the need for further field investigation. Based on the material presented, it may be inferred that the architectural fragments from the Latsithkia area once adorned a temple-like structure dating to the end of the first century CE that was constructed at the site of their discovery. The potential identification of a Greek-style temple from the Early Roman period in Latsithkia calls for a reassessment of architectural developments in Cyprus, possibly suggesting new Roman investment in extra muros sanctuaries.

  2. Shopping Local and Trusted Traders: Identifying Production Zones and Trade Routes of Northern Italian Terra Sigillata Using Similarity Indices
    James Page · pp. 499-520
    摘要

    Italian Terra Sigillata (ITS) was one of the most widely traded fine wares in the Roman world, seeing distribution across the Mediterranean and beyond. Northern Italian Terra Sigillata (NITS) has rarely been included in wider debates surrounding ITS, with its zones of production, consumption, and trade routes still matters of speculation. To better understand how NITS was produced and traded during the first century BCE and first century CE, an assemblage of 1,844 legible stamps on NITS from 24 sites in Northern Italy was analyzed using the Morisita-Horn Index of similarity. The similarity analysis generated two geographic clusters of sites containing similar NITS stamp assemblages, suggesting that two distinct production zones existed. Further analysis of the stamp assemblages within each cluster demonstrates limited chronological overlap between the two, implying they correspond to different phases of production. This pattern is corroborated by unstamped assemblages. Mapping the distribution of workshop output from each cluster also suggests that this ware was traded over short and long distances by different mechanisms. Within Northern Italy, it was primarily consumed a short distance from where it was produced, with little regional circulation. In contrast, output from both identified production zones had similar long-distance distribution to sites in the Balkans.

  3. Asking Different Questions: 3D Scanning and the Refinement of the Typological Method in Hadrianic Portraiture
    Julia Lenaghan, Lee Ann Riccardi · pp. 521-545
    摘要

    This study aims to identify imperial portraits through contextual and iconographic markers rather than from physiognomy and hairstyle. Modern scholarship categorizes portraits into types based on facial features and hair arrangements, applying standards facilitated by photography, a mid 19th-century invention that fundamentally altered expectations with regard to physical resemblance. The approach implies that ancient viewers recognized emperors through a facial likeness and fixed hairstyle. Using 3D scanning of seven Hadrianic-period sculptural portraits, analysis of provincial coinage, and a text by Arrian, we demonstrate that this methodology, while productive for identifying some relationships, does not fully describe how Roman imperial imagery functioned. Arrian explicitly states that schema (form/iconography) and location mattered more than a physical resemblance. Similarly, provincial coins of Hadrian display a range of physiognomic variations, thereby leaving the inscriptions and imperial context to indicate identity. Digital technology offers new tools in this regard: superimposition reveals that while some portraits demonstrate direct copying, others assigned to the same purported “type” share loose patterns. The evidence indicates a flexible system in which imperial symbolism and context took precedence over physiognomic likeness, with significant implications for how we identify portraits and understand Roman visual communication.

  4. Divus Hadrianus, Divus Constantius Chlorus, and the Apotheosis of Hercules: The Tondo Reliefs of the Arch of Constantine
    Susan Wood · pp. 547-573
    摘要

    The second-century CE relief tondi now on the fourth-century CE Arch of Constantine are anomalous both in subject matter and in format. Among these eight reliefs, the scenes of the lion hunt and of the sacrifice to Hercules differ strikingly from the others, as well as from conventional iconography. The sacrifice to Hercules shows the image of the hero as an apparition in the heavens rather than a statue on a pedestal, a composition that perhaps calls attention to the unique status of Hercules as a mortal who won immortality through his noble deeds, just as worthy Roman emperors received apotheosis. The original patron of the tondi, probably Antoninus Pius, may have sought to justify the controversial deification of his predecessor Hadrian. The later reuse of the reliefs on the arch to honor Constantine similarly invoked the deification of his father, Constantius Chlorus, in support of Constantine’s claim to power.

  5. Castellaraccio di Monteverdi (Civitella Paganico, Grosseto): A Deserted Medieval Village of the Ardenghesca
    Alessandro Sebastiani, Alessandro Carabia, Cleo Barbafiera, Massimo De Benetti, Marta De Pari, Matteo Penco et al. · pp. 575-618
    摘要

    This article presents the results of six years of archaeological excavations at Castellaraccio di Monteverdi, a minor fortified settlement in the middle valley of the Ombrone River in southern Tuscany. Previously known almost exclusively through 12th- and 13th-century CE documentary sources, the site now provides a tightly dated archaeological sequence that permits a reassessment of the foundation, function, and abandonment of a short-lived hilltop center within a contested territorial landscape. Excavations conducted by the IMPERO Project document episodic use of the hilltop in the Hellenistic period and possibly the early Middle Ages, with no evidence of structural or demographic continuity. The data place the formal establishment of the castle between the late 11th and early 12th centuries CE, followed by architectural investment, spatial reorganization, and economic specialization focused on storage and infrastructural control, including a bridge across the Ombrone River. Stratigraphic, architectural, and environmental evidence show that Castellaraccio was not a marginal or failed settlement but a selectively invested seigneurial site rendered redundant by planned territorial reorganization, particularly the foundation of the borgo franco of Paganico in 1292. Sporadic 14th-century reuse complicates narratives of abrupt abandonment and highlights archaeology’s role in reassessing models of incastellamento in medieval Tuscany.1

  6. Mycenaeans in Messenia: Princes of Pylos, from Kalamata to Los Angeles and Athens
    Sarah P. Morris · pp. 619-631
    摘要

    New discoveries from Mycenaean Pylos in southwest Greece were the focus of an exhibition inaugurated in early 2025 at the Archaeological Museum of Messenia (Kalamata). This exhibition traveled to the J. Paul Getty Museum Villa in Los Angeles in June of the same year for an expanded display, before returning to Greece in 2026 to be on view at the Hellenic National Archaeological Museum in Athens. The exhibition was unusual for its generous display of recently excavated material from tombs near the Palace of Nestor at Pylos. These tombs complemented selections from 10 Bronze Age sites in Messenia that preceded and were absorbed into Nestor’s kingdom. Together with a catalogue of essays and illustrations, the exhibition integrated archaeological excavation with digital documentation, historical and art historical interpretation, and scientific analysis of human remains. The results offer lasting lessons on the prehistoric Aegean (Bronze Age Greece).

  7. Alexandreia Troas in the Hellenistic Period: War, Finance, and Civic Identity
    Christina DiFabio
    摘要

    This monograph is a significant contribution concerning Hellenistic kingdoms' effects on cities' and local communities' responses.Focusing on Alexandreia Troas in the Troad of western Asia Minor, Ellis-Evans shows how numismatic evidence can provide a more nuanced understanding of kings' urban foundations.Strabo, who cites Demetrius of Skepsis, says that Antigonus Monophthalmus first created Antigoneia Troas through a synoikism (ca.311-306 BCE) and that Lysimachus renamed it Alexandreia Troas (301 BCE).Typically, it has been assumed that the six communities discussed by Strabo (Skepsis, Kebren, Neandreia, Kolonai, Larisa, and Hamaxitos) were part of the original foundation.Dependent settlements, however, would not have been allowed to mint their own coins.Through die studies and redating of certain series of coinage, Ellis-Evans demonstrates that the physical size, imperial relations, and civic identity of Alexandreia Troas fluctuated over time.The book is organized into two parts: Part I has 11 chapters that provide the historical narrative of Alexandreia from its Hellenistic foundation until its life under the Roman emperor Commodus; part II is the numismatic catalogue.Chapter 1, the introduction, outlines the city's important location near the Hellespont and makes the case for using numismatic evidence since the site was spoliated and most surviving epigraphic and archaeological evidence comes from the Augustan colony.Chapter 2 presents the synoikism, which is the foundation for the rest of the book.Strabo only definitively says that Kebren and Skepsis were part of the original synoikism, and he notes that Lysimachus removed Skepsis (Strabo 13.1.26,33, 47, 52).Ellis-Evans suggests that the initial foundation included four settlements (Skepsis, Kebren, Neandreia, and Kolonai).Alexandreia adopted the grazing horse from Neandreia's coinage by 300 BCE, and Neandreia was not occupied past the late fourth century BCE.No evidence for an independent Kolonai makes its participation likely.Hamaxitos and Larisa appear not to be part of the foundation, since contemporary decrees of Hamaxitos and Antigoneia exist, and Larisa Alexandreia Troas in the Hellenistic Period: War, Finance, and Civic Identity

  3. Editorial: Rewriting archaeology with AI
    Robin Skeates · pp. 1191-1201 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Frontispiece 1. Ancient Greek computing technology: the Antikythera mechanism, on display at the Hellenic National Archaeological Museum in Athens (inv.no.X 15087).This 2000-year-old device is claimed to be the world's first analogue computer.It was retrieved in 1901 from an ancient shipwreck off the Greek island of Antikythera.This fragmentary and corroded, box-shaped bronze device originally measured about 340mm high.Inscriptions identified on it support the consensus that it was constructed to predict the positions of celestial bodies.It featured several interlinked indicators, driven by a system of interlocking gearwheels, which displayed the positions of the moon and the sun and likely the planets within the zodiac, the date according to the Egyptian calendar, a Greek lunisolar calendar, and details of upcoming solar and lunar eclipses.Modern research, including a new digital simulation, suggests that the mechanism was prone to errors.It could only be cranked to about four months into the future before its complex system of interconnected gears jammed or its teeth disengaged and everything needed to be reset.Is there a lesson here for modern computing technology?Photograph: Francesco Bini.CC BY-SA 4.0.

  4. The introduction of fish to the Scandinavian mountains by hunter-fisher-gatherers 5000 BC
    Axel Mjærum, Ellen K. Friis, Trygve Hesthagen, Andreas J. Kirchhefer · pp. 1202-1218 · online 2026-07-20
    摘要

    Deglaciation of the Fennoscandian Ice Sheet ( c . 8000 BC) opened the mountainous areas of inland southern Norway for human habitation, but topographic barriers prevented the migration of fish into upland watercourses. Dates from four fish traps found in the exposed bed of Lake Tesse, within the central mountainous region of Norway, are reported here, with the earliest (at around 5000 BC) indicating that Mesolithic hunter-fisher-gatherers were exploiting fish that could have been present within the lake only through human-mediated introduction. These results help illuminate an early example of environmental management, and perhaps even a form of food production, among hunter-fisher-gatherers.

  5. Destruction and resilience at Panaztepe: archaeoseismological evidence for third-millennium BC earthquakes in Western Anatolia
    Ümit Gündoğan, Ümit Çayır Tığlı · pp. 1219-1238 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    Earthquakes and the destruction they wreak on communities and landscapes are regular features of both modern news output and historical accounts. Archaeology can add to our understanding of such disasters, demonstrated here in the discussion of architectural damage noted during recent excavations at Panaztepe, an Early Bronze Age settlement in Western Anatolia. Distinct destruction horizons illustrate the primary and secondary impacts of two earthquakes during the third millennium BC: the first was followed by reconstruction and adaptation, the second by abandonment. By focusing on evidence of seismic activity, the authors examine the resilience of communities inhabiting this geologically fragile region.

  6. Inequality declined in the Bronze Age city of Mohenjo-daro
    Adam S. Green, Iqtedar Alam, Cameron Petrie · pp. 1239-1254 · online 2026-05-18
    摘要

    Mohenjo-daro was a major city of the Indus Civilisation ( c . 2600–1900 BC), with excavations revealing evidence for public infrastructure, civic amenities and hundreds of residences. Archaeologists traditionally assume that urbanism is accompanied by economic stratification, but, at Mohenjo-daro, qualitative evidence of inequality is absent. Drawing on early excavation data, the authors here calculate Gini coefficients of residence area, providing a quantitative proxy of economic inequality. Their results indicate that Gini coefficients, and thus inequality, declined over time, coinciding with increased prosperity and the development of the city’s street plan, indicating that governance likely helped limit economic inequality.

  7. Road to encounters: stelae, burial practices and cultural hybridisation at Las Capellanías, Spain
    Timoteo Rivera Jiménez, Marta Díaz-Guardamino, David W. Wheatley, José Antonio Lozano Rodríguez, Raquel Montero Artús, Lucy Shaw Evangelista et al. · pp. 1255-1276 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    Although thousands of prehistoric standing stones have been recorded across Iberia, the lack of associated detailed contextual data allows ongoing debate about their possible functions. Following the chance discovery of a ‘diademated’ stela in 2018, excavations at Las Capellanías (Cañaveral de León, Huelva), in south-west Spain, led to the discovery of two more stelae and facilitated the compilation of vital contextual information. Here, the authors explore the association between these stelae, Bronze and Iron Age burials and an ancient routeway, revealing reuse, temporal persistence, geographical connectivity and cultural hybridisation in the widening networks of prehistoric Iberia.

  8. An early lead-smelting tradition in north-east Iberia: a short-lived innovation of the third millennium BC
    Julia Montes-Landa, Andreu Moya i Garra, Natàlia Alonso, Marcos Martinón-Torres · pp. 1277-1292 · online 2026-07-10
    摘要

    The polymetallic origins and non-linear development of metallurgy have only relatively recently attracted more scholarly attention. Within this framework, the identification of three lead-smelting slag nodules in early second-millennium BC contexts at Minferri (Catalonia, Spain) provides what is currently the earliest direct evidence of lead smelting in Iberia. These materials are linked to previous indirect evidence of lead smelting in the area from the early third millennium BC. Despite the persistence of lead smelting across the Pyrenees in southern France, it was abandoned in north-east Iberia after the mid-second millennium BC, highlighting divergent sociotechnological pathways even in areas of close cultural contact.

  9. In the heat of the furnaces: 300 years of metalmaking at Bronze Age Taldysai, central Kazakhstan
    Ilaria Calgaro, Antonina Sergeevna Yermolayeva, Miljana Radivojević · pp. 1293-1311 · online 2026-07-10
    摘要

    High-resolution archaeometallurgical analysis of production debris offers a direct means of reconstructing ancient metalmaking traditions, yet remains underexplored relative to the study of finished artefacts. By integrating slag morphology, microstructure and compositional data from Late Bronze Age Taldysai, this study reconstructs, for the first time in Eurasian Steppe archaeology, a continuous 300-year tradition of copper-alloy production. The results reveal technological continuity alongside innovation, including advanced furnace designs, shared metallurgical knowledge and episodes of experimentation. The authors state that these findings position local semipastoralist communities not as peripheral adopters but as active innovators at the heart of Bronze Age Eurasian metallurgical developments.

  10. Haughey’s Fort: a major complex of power, production and ritual in Late Bronze Age Europe
    James O’Driscoll, Patrick Gleeson · pp. 1312-1329 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    Before Navan Fort became the mythological capital of the Irish province of Ulster, construction of the monumental Haughey’s Fort around 1200 BC marked the foundation of an important prehistoric centre. Here, the authors report on research integrating remote sensing, excavation and archival reassessment in the exploration of this Bronze Age landscape. Indicators of long-distance exchange and craft production of high-status artefacts, together with the presence of 204 possible structures and a ceremonial avenue leading towards a unique ritual pool, help elucidate the site’s social, economic and ritual importance, while identification of a 109ha enclosure underlines the exceptional scale of activities.

  11. A major monastic centre? Rethinking Lismore’s place in the ecclesiastical landscapes of early medieval Britain
    Clare Ellis, Angela Boyle, Gemma Cruickshanks, Nicholas Evans, Robert Hay, Edouard Masson-MacLean et al. · pp. 1330-1349 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Until recent decades, the monastery of Lios Mòr (Lismore) in Argyll, Scotland, established by Saint Mo Luóc in the sixth century, was attested only via historical references and local traditions. Community-led investigations have now revealed an early medieval cemetery, sophisticated craftworking of international importance and early structures around the island’s parish church. These discoveries underscore the site’s status as a high-ranking ecclesiastical centre of the Insular north-west and elucidate its significance and integral role within the early Christian networks of Dál Riata, offering insights into the material traces of early medieval monasticism in northern Britain in the first millennium AD.

  12. The anonymous protagonist? Agency and abstraction in Kyushu decorated tombs of the Japanese Late Kofun period
    Claudia Zancan · pp. 1350-1368 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    Mortuary traditions offer a wealth of insights into the social landscapes of human communities. Here, the author examines anthropomorphic representations in the decorated tombs of Kyushu Island, Japan, dating from the Late Kofun period (sixth–seventh centuries AD). The consistently subordinate scale, strategic architectural placement and systematic anonymity of these figures suggest that they did not function as portraits of the dead but were likely symbolic agents embodying a collective rather than individual identity. By demonstrating that the human figure is not consistently the protagonist in these funerary contexts, this study reframes our understandings of Late Kofun deathways.

  13. The identification and work of an eighth-century Maya mathematician
    Franco D. Rossi, David Stuart, Heather Hurst · pp. 1369-1384 · online 2026-07-14
    摘要

    Maya glyphic texts from the Classic period (250–900 CE) typically chronicle the exploits of historical or divine characters; everyday or functional records are rare. Here, the authors offer a reconstruction and transcription of a ‘microtext’ painted on an interior wall of Structure 10K-2 at the site of Xultun, Guatemala. The text records a unique astronomical formula that concludes with a name, attributing the work to an individual named Sak Tahn Waax (‘White-chested Fox’). To date, this is the only known example of a Classic Maya mathematician directly credited for their work, attesting to the value of its intellectual authorship.

  14. Brass technology transmission via maritime routes: crucible evidence from southern China
    Hongyan Xiao, Xinzhen Yue, Zaoxin Li, Yefeng Liu, Jianfeng Cui · pp. 1385-1403 · online 2026-07-20
    摘要

    Despite descriptions in tenth-century-AD written accounts, direct evidence for early brass production in China is scarce. The discovery of crucibles and a furnace dating to the ninth–tenth centuries in Guangzhou, southern China, is therefore a key find. Analysis of metal residues found within these crucibles, presented here, confirms the end-to-end processing of raw materials and production of brass via cementation. The authors argue that this find provides a new perspective on the transmission of brass-making knowledge and technology, highlighting the potential roles of institutionalised industry and maritime routes in the emergence of localised brass production in medieval southern China.

  15. Multiproxy analysis reveals migration and potential origins of the first Christians in medieval Vilnius
    Giedrė Motuzaitė Matuzevičiūtė, Rytis Jonaitis, Irma Kaplūnaitė, Rūta Brindzaitė, Marine Morvan, Christophe Snoeck et al. · pp. 1404-1420 · online 2026-07-15
    摘要

    Before Catholicism was officially adopted in AD 1387, the Lithuanian city of Vilnius was assumed to be largely pagan. Identification of Christian burials within a large medieval cemetery (late thirteenth–fourteenth centuries) in the city was therefore unexpected. Here, the authors employ multiproxy isotopic analyses of bone collagen, dentine and enamel to examine the origins of 15 individuals from the cemetery. Based on the results, the authors argue that males were more socially mobile than females, with one male possibly having immigrated from south-eastern Europe, supporting historical accounts of a multiethnic founder community including immigrant Orthodox Christians and local converts.

  16. Before and after the Black Death: understanding catastrophic and attritional mortality
    Emilia R. Franklin, John Robb, Piers D. Mitchell · pp. 1421-1436 · online 2026-06-11
    摘要

    Despite common associations with plague pits and mass graves, many individuals who succumbed to the Black Death in the mid-fourteenth century AD, and to the numerous subsequent outbreaks of plague, were buried in parish cemeteries. This limits the usefulness of contemporaneous cemetery assemblages for the establishment of comparative, non-catastrophic (or ‘attritional’) demographic profiles for medieval populations in Britain. Drawing from three earlier cemeteries, this study reveals similarities in age-at-death and survivorship profiles for adults, offering a characteristic pre-Black Death profile that helps reinforce the distinctiveness of the over-representation of individuals aged 15–24 years among plague burials.

  17. Heritage forfeited and forgotten: some issues with state control in Tanzania
    Elgidius B. Ichumbaki, Peter R. Schmidt · pp. 1437-1454 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    Central governments have controlled cultural heritage management in most African countries since colonial times. Focusing on Tanzania, the authors here argue that heritage sites are at risk from the institutions tasked with managing them, citing an institutional reluctance to protect threatened sites, delegation of responsibility to unqualified organisations, inappropriate development of sites as income-generating entities and a resistance to working with and recognising local community initiatives. The deleterious impacts of such management are explored through four case studies, which the authors hope will help sound an alarm in the international community and provoke calls for a national review of policy.

  28. Surgical anaesthesia in Ming China: scientific analysis of aconitine residues on medical instruments – CORRIGENDUM
    Xue Ling, Jingyu Li, Ge Zhao, Xu Cao, Xuehua Weng, Hong Zhang et al. · pp. 1496-1497 · online 2026-06-23

  1. Silk and sacrifice at Sanxingdui: early evidence of silk use in ancient Shu, south-west China
    Jianbo Guo, Ruochen Ge, Qiutong Cai, Jingyu Tian, Lu Yang · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    Organic materials are rare but important components of the archaeological record. Here, the authors analyse textile residues adhering to artefacts excavated at Sanxingdui in Sichuan Province, China, a site associated with the ancient Shu culture. Through combined consideration of fibre characteristics, colouring practices, weaving structures and excavation contexts, the authors argue that, by the Late Shang period (1300–1046 BC), silk was in large-scale use at the site and, together with bronze, jade and ivory artefacts, was an important component of the Sanxingdui sacrificial system. The findings help situate ancient Shu within the broader trajectory of early Chinese silk traditions.

  8. China’s 2025 ‘Top 10 Archaeological Discoveries’
    A. Mark Pollard, Wei Wang, Ruiliang Liu · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-10-21
    摘要

    Although China has a long tradition of historical writing, antiquarian scholarship and collecting, archaeology as a modern discipline is comparatively young, and yet the scale and volume of modern fieldwork in China probably now outstrip those in any other country.Every year for more than 30 years, the archaeology sector in China has selected 10 archaeological sites as its 'Top 10 Archaeological Discoveries in China', popularly known as the 'Oscars of Chinese Archaeology'.Founded in 1990 as an initiative between the Chinese Archaeological Society and the bi-weekly newspaper China Cultural Relics News (中国文物报), the selection process was upgraded in 2009 to be supervised by the National Cultural Heritage Administration (an administrative agency affiliated with the Ministry of Culture and Tourism of the People's Republic of China), establishing it as a highly authoritative industry benchmark.In 2019, the selection regulations were revised, and the approval process was further standardised.Projects can be either archaeological research programmes or excavations conducted during infrastructure construction, and they are nominated by the organisations themselves.Decisions are announced annually in April, covering the previous calendar year.Our aim here is to bring this internationally important competition to a wider audience through a description of the selection process and a summary of this year's 10 sites, rather than to provide comments on the final selection.A panel of more than 100 council members of the Chinese Archaeological Society assess the applications and create a short list of 20 projects, which are then evaluated by a committee of around 21 senior archaeologists.This process requires excavation team leaders to deliver a 15-minute presentation followed by a Q&A session; these are now livestreamed across multiple national and social-media platforms, allowing the public to follow professional archaeological debate in real time.The scale of this engagement can be considerable: when the final-selection process moved entirely online in 2020, the livestream attracted 22.78 million cumulative views by the morning of the second day, after a full day and a half of project presentations, subsequently exceeding 40 million views following the final announcement.This openness not only strengthens professional scrutiny and accountability, but also turns the selection into a major annual event for public engagement, stimulating wider interest in archaeological research and heritage protection in China.Importantly, the nomination process often precedes the publication in formal or traditional channels for major excavations, so it is an extremely useful way of keeping abreast of the most recent finds for both Chinese and international scholars.

  10. Crafting power in Kush: faience production and state control in the Napatan region
    Rennan Lemos, Kathryn Howley, Siobhan Shinn, Shadia Abdu Rabo · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-19
    摘要

    Sanam Temple lies in the heart of Napata, the capital of the Kushite Empire in the first millennium BCE. Excavations at the temple (2018–2020) have revealed the remains of a mid-seventh-century BCE faience-production workshop, comprising three firing pits, two mudbrick basins and various faience objects, numerous beads, small shells and wasters found in compacted ashy layers.   11. Ceramic petrography in Early Òṣogbo: craft technology, regional exchanges and social convergence
    Bongumenzi Nxumalo, Akinwumi Ogundiran · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-10-19
    摘要

    Excavations at Early Òṣogbo, a frontier market town in south-western Nigeria occupied c . AD 1590–1725, recovered ceramics associated with the Ọ̀ṣun, Ọ̀yọ́ and Ifẹ̀ traditions. To understand how these styles converged, the authors undertook thin-section petrographic analysis of 43 ceramic samples to examine clay composition. The results, they argue, show that potters across a 150km-long region consistently used micaceous kaolinitic clays from alluvial corridors. These clays, low in porosity and resistant to thermal stress, were well-suited for production of culinary wares and smoking pipes. Their use demonstrates knowledge circulation and technological borrowing through political expansion, mobility and trade networks.

  12. Eating under Christian rule: isotopic evidence for food access and resilience among a medieval Muslim minority in Iberia

    摘要

  17. Social dynamics of toxic metal industry in medieval Europe: Peak District lead production, AD 600–1500

    摘要

    The Derbyshire Peak District in central England was one of the largest lead-producing regions in medieval Europe, yet the early history of production is obscure. Here, the authors use tooth-enamel-based lead concentrations accumulated in childhood in three cemeteries, linked to radiocarbon dates, to identify the development of post-Roman lead smelting from the mid-seventh century, with large-scale production and contamination around key mines, including Castleton, by the mid-eighth century AD.   2. Shell, deer, and the postapocalyptic village: Transmotion and Indigenous futurity at Puhú
    Nathan Acebo · pp. 217-242 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    At Puhú (CA-ORA-132/317), a village in the Santa Ana Mountains of southern California, shell and other more-than-human relations organized village life. This article interprets these relations through local forms of transmotion, Gerald Vizenor’s concept of transcendent Indigenous movement rooted in ancestral relations, story, and place. Based on 10 years of collaborative research with Tongva, Acjachemen, and Payómkawichum (Pechanga Band of Indians) communities through the Black Star Canyon Archaeological Project (2013–2020), this article explores how shell, transported inland from the coast and embedded in the mountain fossilized deposits, supported conditions of Indigenous futurity in communal prosperity, governance, ceremony, and autonomy. A vignette presents these relations as speculative storytelling to further explore the senses of transmotion offered by the archaeological record. The article then examines how archaeologies of Indigenous futurity risk imposing a discovery logic. Instead, long-term, community-driven collaboration acknowledges futurity as already present, requiring no analytical naming or genre tropes.

  3. Glocalization and the urbanization of Roman Gaul
    Aaron W. Irvin · pp. 243-263 · online 2026-08-25
    摘要

    The emergence of urbanism in Iron Age Gaul has traditionally been divided into two separate narratives: developments up to the Gallic oppida prior to Roman conquest, and the appearance of the Gallo-Roman civitates under the Empire. Recent scholarship has further complicated this picture by demonstrating a much longer history of urbanization throughout temperate Europe, demonstrating that settlement agglomeration, economic specialization, political centralization, and monumentalization developed through repeated cycles extending from the Hallstatt period to the Roman conquest, with no single clear trajectory towards urbanism. However, this new scholarship has largely described these cycles rather than analyzing and explaining why settlements collapsed, or why they stabilized under Roman domination. This article argues that glocalization provides a framework for understanding these long-term processes. Glocalization allows the oppida and civitates to be understood as successive stages within a continuous process. Through a comparative analysis of settlements across the Three Gauls, this article demonstrates how the Gallic elite selectively appropriated Roman political institutions, monumental architecture, and imperial imagery to effectively articulate and stabilize their local authority. Roman rule did not introduce urbanism into Gaul but provided a broader framework through which long durée processes of political experimentation acquired stable, durable expression.

  4. Locating story in place: The intersection of cultural landscapes and archaeology on the Northwestern Plains
    Lindsay Amundsen-Meyer, Kent Ayoungman, Jerry Potts · pp. 264-288 · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    Archaeology in North America occurs in a settler-colonial context, wherein a significant discrepancy exists between Western and Indigenous concepts of cultural heritage. Through examination of the Blackfoot cultural landscape, with its embedded places and stories, alongside the archaeological record of the Northwestern Plains, we attempt to address this gap between archaeology as historically practiced and the descendant community’s experience. Using a series of case studies from the Blackfoot homeland, we demonstrate how viewing archaeological sites within their broader landscape context can identify intangible cultural heritage, foster cultural connection, and reclaim places for descendant communities, ultimately contributing to reconciliation. These outcomes are particularly valuable to Indigenous descendant communities today, as rematriation efforts expand the return of cultural heritage beyond ancestral remains and belongings. We argue that through archaeology, Blackfoot communities can repatriate landscapes by re-establishing the stories and named places traditionally embedded across the landscape.

  5. Archaeology at war: Russia and its hybrid heritage politics
    Gertjan Plets · pp. 289-319 · online 2026-09-26
    摘要

    This paper explores how archaeology functions within Russia’s modern hybrid warfare framework. Journal of Islamic Archaeology

Vol. 13, No. 1 · 2026-09-03 · 5 篇 · Equinox · Islamic world · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Documenting Islamic Coin Finds in Conflict Zones: Provenance, Community, and the Syrian Case
    Alaa Aldin Al Chomari · pp. 1-12 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This article presents a methodological framework for documenting and analysing Islamic coin finds in conflict-affected regions, arguing that for copper coinage (fulūs) provenance constitutes the primary source of historical value rather than a supplementary attribute. Using Syria as a case study, where excavation and institutional recording have been severely constrained since 2011, the article demonstrates how reliable numismatic data can still be generated under conditions of conflict. Based on fieldwork conducted between 2020 and 2023, the study integrates numismatic analysis with a purpose-built digital documentation system designed in an earlier project phase, alongside community-based reporting and ethnographic observation. The digital database, developed to be usable by non-specialists, allows consistent recording of typology, physical parameters, and findspot information under unstable field conditions. Particular attention is given to single finds reported by local communities, especially children, who are often the first discoverers of low-value copper coins whose significance lies in their findspots. Three case studies from al-Raḥba, Ḥalab, and Ḥimṣ demonstrate how securely documented finds can correct misattributions, refine mint histories, and reconstruct micro-regional circulation patterns absent from museum-based datasets. The article proposes a transferable, ethically grounded model for Islamic archaeology and heritage documentation in conflict zones. The study contributes to current debates on archaeological methodology and heritage protection by demonstrating how locally grounded documentation strategies can preserve contextual knowledge under conditions of conflict.

  2. A Jewellery Hoard from Borsippa
    Sandra Heinsch · pp. 13-53 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    In 1981, an assemblage of 44 artefacts was discovered at Tell Ibrahim al-Khalil within the ancient urban area of Borsippa, Iraq. The collection comprises a diverse range of objects, including gold and copper-alloy jewellery, beads, and other ornaments made from precious and semi-precious materials, found in close association with a ceramic vessel and textile fragments. The contextual evidence, including the absence of an identifiable settlement or burial setting, indicates an intentional deposition within medieval occupation layers. The hoard is characterised by considerable heterogeneity in material composition and manufacturing techniques. Technological and typo-chronological analyses indicate that the majority of the artefacts were produced between the 10th and 11th centuries AD, while some may date to the 12th century. Several objects display typological and stylistic features comparable to Fatimid jewellery traditions, with close parallels from Egypt, the southern Levant, Tunisia, Iran, and Syria, while others point to a broader range of regional traditions. The unusual combination of precious and base metals differs from patterns observed in certain Fatimid hoards and instead points to diverse practices of assembling and retaining valuables. As a rare medieval hoard, and currently the only assemblage of its kind known from the Babylon region, the find provides important insights into jewellery production, artisanal practices, and the circulation of materials, forms, and technologies across the medieval Islamic world. Rather than representing a single regional or workshop tradition, the assemblage brings together objects reflecting both local and supra-regional traditions. Its significance lies precisely in this combination, offering a rare glimpse into the material culture of the medieval Babylonian region and its wider connections. Although the precise circumstances and motivation of the deposition remain unclear, the religious significance of Borsippa offers an important interpretative context. The deliberate concealment of valuable objects may have involved personal, social, or symbolic considerations, while a pragmatic response to insecurity cannot be excluded. The hoard thus sheds light not only on the use and concealment of valuables, but also on the changing material and cultural landscape of Borsippa during the medieval period.

  3. Filling the Gap: The Qasr al-Hallabat Wellhead and its Relevance to Establish a Chrono–Typological Sequence in the Transition from Classical Antiquity to Medieval Islamic Period
    Ignacio Arce · pp. 54-78 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This paper presents the contribution made by the recently discovered Umayyad wellhead from Qasr al-Hallabat to the completion of a chrono-typological sequence of wellheads in the transition from Classical Antiquity to the Islamic period, specifically for types that use architectural elements to articulate their decoration. This recently discovered wellhead would fill a sequential gap between the well-defined Classical types, with a rather homogeneous evolution until Late Antiquity, and those developed later during the Islamic period in Al-Andalus, where we once again have a rather complete chrono-typological sequence from the 10th to the 15th century CE. In this paper, we will attempt to bridge these two distinct sequences by means of this new example with the diachronical approach of long durèe.

  4. An Ayyubid Sgraffito Bowl from ʿAsqālan (Ashqelon, Israel): On the Evolution of Medieval Egyptian Glazed Wares and a Glimpse into the City’s Geopolitical Pendulum Swing between Muslims and Crusaders
    Itamar Taxel · pp. 79-95 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    This study discusses a rather unusual Ayyubid-period (late 12th century) glazed sgraffito-decorated bowl of Egyptian origin, which was found in ʿAsqālan (Ashqelon), on the southern Mediterranean coast of Israel. The ʿAsqālan bowl (and its few published counterparts) is chronologically and typologically placed between two major and much more renowned classes of Egyptian glazed wares—the Fustat Fatimid Sgraffito (FFS) and the Mamluk Barracks Sgraffito Ware—and it seems to constitute, respectively, a successor and a predecessor of these two wares. In addition, the study of the ʿAsqālan bowl against the local historical and archaeological background shows that this find provides a rare glimpse into the city’s Ayyubid past, a short but crucial phase in the mid-12th to mid-13th century geopolitical pendulum swing during which the city switched hands from Muslims to Crusaders and vice versa.

  5. Revisiting Islamic Archaeology in the Malay World: With Special Attention to the Correct Date of Batu Aceh Permatang Pasir in Pahang
    Amru Sazali · pp. 96-112 · online 2026-09-03
    摘要

    Islamic archaeology is a discipline largely underexplored by archaeologists in maritime Southeast Asia. Few specialists have directed sustained attention to the discipline, perhaps due to the perception that, as Islam continues to be a living tradition today, and Islamic history is relatively recent, compared to the likes of Srivijaya, Majapahit, or other Hindu-Buddhist traditions. Consequently, Islamic artefacts have often been misread or poorly dated, leading to chronological confusion and the proliferation of questionable historical narratives. In some cases, such errors are compounded by ideological motives or selective readings shaped by political or group interests. This study particularly focuses on one such artefact that has remained unresolved for over four decades—the Batu Aceh Permatang Pasir in Pahang. Some researchers claimed it dates to 419 AH/1028 AD, identifying it as the oldest Islamic tombstone in the Malay world. Despite ongoing suspicion surrounding its form and script, no one has yet provided a conclusive and convincing answer to the correct date of the artefact. This paper argues for the need to apply rigorous, methodologically grounded approaches to the dating of such artefacts, which, once put in place, could resolve many problems surrounding artefactual dates. Hence, this will result in a proper chronology and historical narrative that reflects what has truly occurred in reality in the past. This research finds that Batu Aceh Permatang Pasir, through detailed epigraphic analysis, comparative typology, and contextual palaeography, the correct date of the inscription is 914 AH, equivalent to 1508 AD. This is based on the outlined methodology and arguments. By situating the tombstone within a broader corpus of Islamic material culture in maritime Southeast Asia, this paper contributes to the ongoing debates on Islamisation, the standardisation of Islamic epigraphy, and the cultural dynamics of the Indian Ocean world.

  1. A Review of the Turkwel Tradition: Cultural Variability and Social Change in the First Millennium CE East Africa
    Nik Petek-Sargeant · pp. 367-402 · online 2026-06-05
    摘要

    Collating all available information, literature, data, and research history on the Turkwel tradition and the material, narratives, and interpretations that have been linked to it, the paper reviews the similarities, differences, and nuances of the assemblages and sites associated with the 1st millennium CE tradition found across South Sudan, Ethiopia, Kenya, and Uganda. It discusses issues and shortcomings in how we treat the distinctive grooved pottery as a fossil directeur , but also highlights the area-specific localizations in material culture, production processes, and subsistence that make this tradition so variable. Through the analysis focused on ceramics, it also proposes a division of Turkwel into two phases and a northern and southern facies, links the former typological isolate to South Sudan’s Lokabulo tradition, and points to new avenues of research that could shed light on social dynamics in the 1st millennium CE and the Pastoral Neolithic-Pastoral Iron Age transition. The review proposes to treat the Turkwel tradition and its peoples as bricoleurs, adopting and appropriating new and existing practices and ideas into their culture. The trait might be a symptom of broader socio-cultural and technological developments happening in East Africa at the time that contributed to a flurry of new ceramic wares and which indicates new attitudes towards the “materialization” of identity.

  3. Auditory Affordances of Game Pass Shelter (South Africa): Implications for Performative Practices and Multisensory Engagement with San Rock Art
    Neemias Santos da Rosa, Lidia Alvarez-Morales, Diego Moreno-Iglesias, Ghilraen Laue, Margarita Díaz-Andreu · pp. 437-451 · online 2026-08-19
    摘要

    Sensory research on San rock art has often focused on whether acoustic effects influenced the selection of places to be painted or engraved. This article addresses the issue from a different perspective by examining the auditory affordances of Game Pass shelter, one of the most iconic rock art sites in southern Africa. Using impulse-response measurements adapted from room acoustics, we analyzed reverberation, clarity, definition, and sound strength within the shelter. The results show that Game Pass is acoustically "dry," with low reverberation and local reinforcement of direct sound through early reflections. Rather than producing spectacular sonic effects, the site offers conditions in which sounds involved in performative practices could remain clear while gaining presence at specific listening positions. Although these measurements do not demonstrate that specific performances took place at the shelter, they indicate the kinds of sonic practices Game Pass could have supported. More generally, the study suggests that the acoustic significance of painted shelters may lie less in unusual phenomena than in how their auditory affordances facilitate performances involving sound and contribute to multisensory engagement with rock art.

  4. Breaking Borders: The Medieval Archaeology of the Ethiopian-Somaliland Borderland
    Jorge de Torres Rodríguez, Jorge Rouco Collazo, Valeria Leticia Franco Salvi, Manuel Antonio Franco Fernández, Mohamed Abdi Allamagen, Carolina Cornax Gómez et al. · pp. 453-494 · online 2026-06-25
    摘要

    This article examines the impact of colonial borders on the interpretation of medieval archaeology in the Horn of Africa, focusing on the region now divided between Ethiopia and Somaliland. The boundary established in the 1930s fragmented what had historically been an integrated cultural and economic landscape, leading to uneven research trajectories and hindering the reconstruction of historical processes whose scope transcended modern political limits. Through an archaeological project conducted between 2022 and 2025 in the Aw Bare and Harawo woredas of Ethiopia’s Somali Region, nineteen sites—fifteen previously unknown for the academic literature—were identified and documented, allowing for a reassessment of medieval Muslim settlement patterns between the thirteenth and seventeenth centuries. The results reveal a hierarchical sedentary occupation, ranging from major urban centers such as Aw Bare and Aw Boba to medium-sized villages, small hamlets, and isolated dwellings, alongside defensive structures and specialized cemeteries. Excavations at Aiyanle produced a detailed stratigraphic sequence, imported materials, and radiocarbon dates that place the site’s peak during the rule of the Barr Saʿd al-Dīn Sultanate, followed by a decline associated with its collapse in the late sixteenth century. In parallel, substantial evidence of nomadic occupation was recorded, including ritual aggregation sites, cemeteries, and craft-related activity areas. Together, these data reveal a densely occupied and highly complex landscape in which sedentary and nomadic communities interacted dynamically. The findings also challenge interpretations shaped by contemporary borders and offer new insights into territorial organization, economic strategies, and religious practices in the medieval Muslim Horn of Africa.

  5. Lithic Techno-economics and OSL Chronology of the Later Stone Age at Chessungalane, Save River Valley, Mozambique
    Osama Samawi, Jeffrey I. Rose, Li Li, Ana Gomes, Daniel Martins, Elena Skosey-LaLonde et al. · pp. 495-523 · online 2026-06-06
    摘要

    Nestled between East and Southern Africa, the Stone Age archaeology of Mozambique offers important insights into Later Stone Age (LSA) behavioral variability across a region pivotal to understanding human adaptations. Here, we present results from technological analysis, refitting and optically stimulated luminescence (OSL) dating of the newly discovered site of Chessungalane (CHE) in south-central Mozambique. OSL dates indicate occupation spanning ~ 23–7 ka, corresponding to the early LSA through Wilton periods. Technological analysis of lithic artifacts, refitting, and raw material survey reveals differential technological investment strategies employed by LSA groups. Chert was introduced during last occupation phase for systematic bladelet production through complex reduction sequences, likely due to its scarcity and superior knapping qualities. In contrast, quartz and quartzite were procured during all the occupation phases for simple flake production via shorter, simpler reduction sequences, reflecting their local abundance and fracture constraints. Preliminary comparison with contemporaneous LSA sequences from the broader region suggests that CHE followed a distinct technological trajectory, with bladelet production appearing significantly later in time, highlighting the importance of regional variability in shaping LSA technological behavior. The results demonstrate a strong relationship between raw material availability, quality, and technological investment, and contribute critical new data to the largely undocumented LSA record of Mozambique.

  6. Bored Discs of Stone and Rain Control in Kaingo Game Reserve, Limpopo Province, South Africa
    Lyn Wadley, Ghilraen Laue, Wim Biemond · pp. 525-554 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    Bored disc fragments of quartzitic sandstone were excavated from Welgevonden Rock Shelter and collected from the surface of the talus slope of Dwaalhoek 11 Rock Shelter on Kaingo Game Reserve in the Waterberg. The fragments represent stages of manufacture, breakage, and occasional recycling as grinders. Naturally flat slabs were knapped, often bifacially, to shape discs. Then, small, rounded hollows were pecked to thin the center of both disc faces. Finally, a central hole was pecked and/or punched, probably using a short, narrow stone reamer. Large, sharp-edged discs may have been hafted axes. Small bored discs resemble stone spindle whorls used by Iron Age farmers, but they were probably imitations for ritual purposes. Eiland facies ceramic sherds were also excavated. Since farmers did not live in rock shelters, it seems likely that makers of Eiland ceramics used Welgevonden for working with rain. The performance of drilling discs to make spindle whorls may have been part of the ritual. A rock art motif at a nearby site suggests that the area was also used by Bushmen for working with rain. Conceptually, threads are important for Bushmen and for Iron Age farmers, and both groups link them to rain. Threads spun on spindle whorls are woven into fine cotton for Iron Age chiefs, who are charged with producing rain for their subjects. When Bushman rain specialists trance, they climb threads to access the sky. Here, or from water pits, they use threads to lead rain animals to slaughter.

    Chapurukha Makokha Kusimba · pp. 555-565 · online 2026-07-09

  1. Chronology and Funerary Practices Associated with Antennae Tumuli in Western Sahara
    Joaquim Soler, Helena Ventura, Isaac Rufí, Neus Coromina, Jordi Revelles, Maria Saña et al. · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    This study presents the results of the archaeological excavation of an antennae tumulus in Western Sahara, conducted at Wadi Lawash el Telli. While earlier research across the region had established a broad typological and chronological framework for prehistoric stone funerary monuments, the function and chronology of antennae tumuli had remained undocumented. Two AMS radiocarbon dates place the burial between 4417 and 4100 cal BP, providing the first absolute chronology for this type of monument in the Atlantic Sahara. These results position antennae tumuli at the end of the Late Neolithic sequence of stone funerary constructions, contemporaneous with crescent-shaped tumuli. Beyond confirming their funerary function, this study helps address the scarcity of chronological and cultural data relevant to understanding the development of prehistoric mortuary traditions in the region and offers new perspectives for future comparative studies across the Sahara.

    摘要

  5. Mapping People, Labor, and Movement in the First Decades (1788–1825) of Colonial Parramatta, Australia, with Historical Spatial Data Infrastructure

    摘要

Established in November 1788, Parramatta is the third-oldest British colonial settlement in the former colony of New South Wales, Australia.   6. Islands under Surveillance: Watch Towers, Gendered Schools, and the Emergence of Modernity in the Mangareva Islands, French Polynesia
    James Flexner, Emilie Perez, Moanatea Claret · pp. 814-840 · online 2026-02-10
    摘要

    Modern societies sought to control their populations through programs of mass education and surveillance. The origins of this practice had roots in Europe, but were quickly exported to colonial contexts around the world, including the Pacific Islands. While schooling and surveillance are currently often associated with state institutions, historically Christian missions were also critical to the spread of these phenomena in colonized territories. In the territory that became French Polynesia, the first permanent Catholic missionaries arrived in the Mangareva Islands beginning in 1834. Archaeological survey and excavations focusing on the building program of the missionaries has documented an extensive landscape of nineteenth-century stone buildings constructed both by missionary frères bâtisseurs (builder-brothers) and Mangarevan converts. Included among these buildings are several watch towers facing inward among the islands in the lagoon, and separate boys’ and girls’ school compounds in the islands of Aukena and Mangareva. The spatial organization and materiality of these spaces demonstrate the use of techniques of surveillance and schooling to attempt to control the Mangarevan population, including socializing male and female students for expected gendered roles in Christian society.

  7. Exploring European Memory of the 1897 Lattimer Massacre with Predictive AI
    Paul Shackel, Emma Gruesbeck · pp. 841-862 · online 2026-03-06
    摘要

    Some historical archaeologists work in places colonized by countries whose language is other than that spoken by the researcher. Many of these projects have not consulted the relevant foreign-language literature, which could provide additional context for interpreting their findings. Predictive AI offers the unique advantage of rapidly accessing, translating, and synthesizing foreign language sources that may otherwise remain inaccessible due to language barriers. The following is an example of a little known international labor tragedy that occurred in northeastern Pennsylvania in 1897, when immigrant coal miners went on strike for better pay and working conditions. At least 19 men were killed and 38 wounded, and most of the victims were citizens of the Austro-Hungarian Empire. Much of the incident and its aftermath have been reconstructed using English-language newspapers, court records, and oral histories. Due to language barriers, scholars have not examined how the victims' home countries recorded and perceived the incident. Using predictive AI enables researchers to create workable translations of these perspectives with a level of speed and scale previously unattainable, broadening the historical context of this incident. This approach provides an example for others whose work spans international boundaries and languages.

    摘要

    This study analyzes material evidence from the Murutucu Sugar Cane Mill in the Brazilian Amazon to clarify the socioeconomic structure of plantation life in the nineteenth century. By combining archaeological and historical sources, the research highlights pronounced differences in how glass objects were used by planters versus the enslaved individuals. These findings enhance our understanding of plantation archaeology by shedding light on everyday life and social dynamics within this historical setting.

  3. Multi-Isotope Analyses of Colonial Sheep from the Sydney Region, Australia: Implications for Husbandry Strategies
    Tanja Nussbaumer, Melanie Fillios, Renaud Joannes-Boyau, Matheus C. Carvalho · online 2026-10-10
    摘要

    Stable isotope analyses of faunal remains have demonstrated significant potential to enhance understandings of human-animal relationships during periods of European colonization. Isotopic analyses enable reconstructions of historical livestock management practices, including diet, provenance, and mobility, while also providing insights into past climatic and environmental conditions. This study presents the first application of a multi-isotope approach to archaeological sheep tooth enamel in Australia, using bulk analysis of carbon (δ¹³C), oxygen (δ¹⁸O), and strontium (⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr) isotopes to reconstruct herd management strategies in colonial New South Wales. Isotopic data was obtained from the tooth enamel of 29 early to mid-nineteenth century sheep recovered from an archaeological context on Clarence and Kent Street, Sydney, and compared with two modern Merino sheep of known provenance and inferred diets. δ¹³C analyses indicate that the archaeological sheep consumed a predominantly C 3 plant-based diet, likely comprising both native and sown pastures, consistent with grazing in open woodland and bushy or grass-covered habitats. The wide range of δ¹⁸O values suggests that the sheep experienced diverse environmental conditions during early life, reflecting variation in local climate, altitude, water sources and seasonality. Sequential ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr enamel analyses reveal limited individual mobility but provide evidence for multiregional livestock sourcing. ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr values indicate at least three potential geological provenance zones, most likely within New South Wales, including regions beyond the Sydney Basin, although the possibility of overseas imports cannot be entirely excluded. These results contribute to broader understandings of livestock management, provisioning strategies, and environmental adaptation in early colonial Australia.

  4. The Archaeology of the Transatlantic Trade in Peki, Ghana: Local Responses to Global Encounters in Atlantic West Africa
    Benjamin Kofi Nutor, Kodzo Gavua · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    Archaeological studies of the transatlantic slave trade in West Africa have traditionally focused on coastal and discrete sites of slavery, leaving interior communities comparatively underexplored. This paper advances methodological frameworks for the archaeology of transatlantic trade that move beyond the search for direct material signatures of enslavement to examine the circulation, appropriation, social valuation, and consumption of imported trade goods, and how they shaped political and economic landscapes in Peki, an Ewe-speaking polity in southeastern Ghana. Drawing on archaeological data from sacred sites, burial contexts, and domestic middens, complemented by oral traditions and historical sources, we examine how participation in the Atlantic trade reshaped political authority, consumption practices, and everyday life between the seventeenth and nineteenth centuries. Archaeological materials from Peki, including liquor bottles, tobacco pipes, and European ceramics, demonstrate a shift from elite-controlled access to broader community consumption during the nineteenth century, coinciding with the transition from slave trading to “legitimate commerce.” By situating local material practices in Peki within wider Atlantic political economies, the paper shows how inland African communities actively navigated global economic forces and suggests that the Peki community be regarded as an important node in the network of places connected to heritage tourism and the UNESCO Slave Routes Projects related to the transatlantic trade in Ghana.

Journal of Material Culture

Vol. 31, No. 3 · 2026-09 · 6 篇 · SAGE · Material culture · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Old devices, new meanings: The enduring materiality of outdated media and communication technologies
    Oya Morva · pp. 319-343 · online 2026-06-26
    摘要

    This article explores the enduring presence and shifting meanings of outdated media and communication technologies (MCTs) in everyday life. Based on 30 in-depth interviews conducted in Istanbul, it examines how individuals retain objects such as Walkmans, vinyl records, and early mobile phones – devices no longer widely used, yet still materially and emotionally significant. Rather than approaching these items as nostalgic collectibles, the study reframes retention as a materially mediated practice through which attachment, memory, and critique are enacted. Bringing material culture studies, science and technology studies, and domestication theory into conversation, the article argues that outdated MCTs act as mediators of identity and memory and enable alternative engagements with time, value, and media. Istanbul's urban ecology – marked by repair networks, second-hand circulation, and layered temporalities – further supports these practices. The article contributes to debates on materiality by showing how old devices persist as materially active participants in everyday urban life – culturally re-signified and affectively charged artefacts rather than passive remnants of past use.

  2. Multiple and ordinary: The materialities of polycystic ovary syndrome
    Júlia Karpova · pp. 344-368 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This article focuses on the everyday materialities of polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS) (currently being renamed to polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome). A PCOS is a combination of symptoms that vary from patient to patient, such as absent or irregular ovulation and menstruation, impaired glucose metabolism, excessive body hair or problems with fertility. This article adds to research on menstrual health and chronic disease, which has tended to prioritise tracking technologies and medical devices, by drawing attention to ordinary material objects. Drawing on Science and Technology Studies theorisations of disease and self-care, this article examines in-depth semi-structured interviews with people with PCOS to illuminate how materiality shapes everyday coping with this common yet diverse condition. The major finding of the analysis is that although medical professionals frame PCOS as a coherent whole, patients attend to their individual ‘packs’ of symptoms in their daily efforts to manage the condition.

  3. Civilizational gene of knotting: The craftsmanship of the Uurga on the Mongolian Plateau and the material thinking of nomadic societies
    Haiyan Bao · pp. 369-392 · online 2026-07-15
    摘要

    This study examines the Uurga, a traditional Mongolian lasso pole used for capturing and managing livestock, exploring its material, social, and symbolic significance in nomadic societies. Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork, this research traces how the Uurga has functioned not only as a practical instrument of domestication but also as a mediator of relationships between humans and animals. The study is organized into three parts. The first part clarifies the contexts of use and naming system of the Uurga. The second part provides a detailed account of Uurga-making, including pole-making, rope-making, and the intricate binding and knotting techniques. The third part explores the customs and beliefs associated with the Uurga. The study suggest that the Uurga represents a rich material culture, and as a primary technique for connecting different materials, knotting is its core method and may be foundational to the emergence and development of nomadic civilization. As such, the Uurga exemplifies the material thinking of nomadic societies, offering deep insights into the social, symbolic, and practical dimensions of life on the Mongolian Plateau.

  4. The birth of the astronomical price: An ethnographic study of value and price in the Beijing art market
    Shijun Zhang · pp. 393-421 · online 2026-07-22
    摘要

    This ethnographic study examines the genesis of the astronomical price—known as tianjia —within the Beijing art market and aims to contribute empirically to anthropological debates on the value of art. Focusing on the dynamic interplay between abstract value and monetary price through the lens of practice, it traces how astronomical prices emerge through the coordinated labor of valuation, the discursive and embodied practices of auctioneers and bidders, and the ritual intensification of the auction event itself. By examining the concrete labor involved in the construction of value and the competitive escalation of bids at auction, the study argues that modern auctions are not merely sites where an object's value is formed and its ownership is transferred, but also ritualized stages on which archaic cosmology and modern calculative knowledge are brought into convergence through interpersonal interactions on the auction floor. It is through this convergence, the article suggests, that a form of collective effervescence is generated and astronomical prices come into being.

  5. Unsettling collections, revitalizing culture: The work of art in Indigenous southeastern Australia
    Anna Weinreich, Vicki Couzens · pp. 422-444 · online 2026-08-20
    摘要

    Situated in southeastern Australia, this article examines the pivotal work of Indigenous artists in revitalizing cultural practices and knowledge that colonial violence worked to erase.   6. Material imaginaries of the new iron curtain
    Natalia Magnani, Matthew Magnani, Anatolijs Venovcevs, Richard Fraser, Giuseppe Amatulli · pp. 445-469 · online 2026-08-08
    摘要

    The “social imaginary” has been critiqued for its overly deterministic qualities and resemblance to concepts of culture. Our article interrogates the materiality of the indeterminate at a time of heightened global uncertainty. Furthering a rapid response, material methodology in anthropology, we examine objects, animals, and infrastructures that disrupt and reinscribe border imaginaries in differential and unexpected ways in Sør-Varanger, on the Norwegian border with Russia, one month after the full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, amid emerging international sanctions and national border closures. We show how everyday engagements with various “materials"—souvenirs and monuments, surveyed and commodified species, industrial and transport infrastructures—foster a sense of uncertainty around the (im)permeability of an East-West border. The array of effects that they have on the imagination is unevenly and at times randomly shaped by their materiality. However, these differential effects are also patterned in their indeterminacy, as the scale and temporality of the materials’ movements affect their potential to transform border imaginaries. We thus rethink the material imaginary as simultaneously indeterminate and patterned in dialogue with scalar and temporal dimensions of mobility.

  1. From traps to camps: Ghost fishing, plastic materialities and carceral entrapment
    Magdalena Buchczyk · online 2026-10-29
    摘要

    This article draws on ethnographic research with weavers and fishermen in Sardinia to explore emergent modalities of entrapment. Following fish traps from a Berlin museum collection to the bottom of the lagoon, and contrasting nasse traps made from plant material with synthetic ghost traps submerged in the Gulf of Oristano, I consider how synthetic materials exercise an indiscriminate, chronic agency that extends beyond single objects and designs. Combining material ecology with a critique of fossil capitalism, I develop the concept of carceral entrapment, or a modality of world-making that produces persistent harm beyond human control, in which the durability of plastics dissolves the dramatic nexus that Alfred Gell saw as constitutive of traps. Instead, carceral entrapment operates across scales and through ecological, economic, and corporeal relations, and generates enduring, unpredictable, and pervasive forms of harm. An anthropology of traps, I argue, must attend to these complex agentic relations and their uneven distributions.

  2. Material noise at Cape Coast Castle: The costs of decolonizing heritage
    Xiao Wei · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Taking Cape Coast Castle as a case, this article asks how material remains sustain decolonial memory while unsettling the institutional arrangements that make it possible. Drawing on guided tours, local conversations, material observation, and institutional discussions, it shows how slavery memory is translated into controlled discomfort: powerful, recognizable, yet ordered enough to remain visitable and comfortable. The article argues that material remains are simultaneously docile and unruly: dampness, residues, corrosion, salt, microbial growth, and climatic weathering give emotional scripts sensory density, while the same traces become material noise when they demand cleaning, repair, conservation, funding, and responsibility. Digital heritage intensifies this tension as decolonial efforts to relieve material burdens risk reproducing coloniality through technological and donor dependency. Decolonization is therefore not the overcoming of pain, but a costly, unsettling, and unresolved struggle to live with material noise.

  3. Thinking with material care
    Taru Peltola, Kati Pitkänen · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    Engaging with care theories and perspectives arising from repair studies, this article proposes material care as a conceptual possibility to think with and to interpret material relations leading to overconsumption and wasting. We bring theoretical ideas on material care into conversation with empirical material from Finland, a country in which discarding is made easy with functioning waste management infrastructures. We use the material to gain understanding of what material care means in practice for people in their everyday life. With selected insights from the material gathered through object-centred dialogues, we reflect on care ethics in action, showing how material objects themselves influence people's choices on care and how avoiding care is part of the ethical considerations. We also discuss affective and embodied relations with things and meaning-making and creativity as crucial elements of material care. Our finding is that people do care about their things even though consumerism suggests otherwise, and we propose that this is because material care is significant to people's wellbeing in ways that are rarely addressed in present policy frameworks. The value of thinking with material care lies in the alternative visions for sustainability that it allows to nurture.

  4. Geometric ornament as material knowledge: Ethnogeometric structures of spatial cognition
    Balnur Amantay, Manifa Sarkulova, İhsan Çapcıoğlu · online 2026-10-14
    摘要

    This article proposes a method for analysing recurring geometric relationships in Chalcolithic Botai material culture in northern Kazakhstan. Ornamented artefacts, dwelling architecture and settlement layout are examined without assuming a single symbolic code or historical continuity. The methodology integrates ethnogeometry, Material Engagement Theory, the enactive signification hypothesis and Peircean and material semiotics. Analysis proceeds through four levels of inference, from observable material features to culturally specific interpretations. Results show that comparable geometric relationships are materialised differently through production, object orientation, bodily interaction, movement and spatial organisation. The unit of comparison is not a motif but a geometric relationship arising within a specific material practice. Formal similarity alone cannot establish ritual or cosmological meaning. Comparative cases from Sakha culture and Çatalhöyük are used asymmetrically to distinguish evidence-based conclusions from speculation. The article therefore treats geometric ornament as a form of embodied material knowledge while defining explicit evidential limits for archaeological interpretation.

Journal of Mediterranean Archaeology

Vol. 39, No. 1 · 2026-08-21 · 5 篇 · Equinox · Mediterranean · Double-blind (verified)
  1. Defining the Use of Cultic Space in the Early Iron Age: Tel Abel Beth Maacah as a Test Case
    Matthew Susnow, Nava Panitz-Cohen, Theo Kassebaum, Naama Yahalom-Mack · pp. 3-32 · online 2026-08-21
    摘要

    Numerous studies on the Late Bronze Age to Iron Age (IA) transition in the southern Levant have underscored how IA I (1190–980 BC) society displays evidence of both cultural continuity and discontinuity. This article investigates the role of cult in this process of transition, utilizing evidence revealed at Tel Abel Beth Maacah in the northern Hula Valley. Employing a methodology based on spatial analysis of artifacts, the article aims to establish the nature of cultic practices at the site, and comparison is made to contemporary IA I sites in the region to contextualize processes of continuity and change. A major break within cultic traditions during IA I is identified: groups were no longer beholden to the Bronze Age temple tradition of using demarcated spaces devoted exclusively to cult. Rather, a new type of space emerged, within which both cultic and quotidian activities were conducted. These spaces were neither standardized in their plans nor did they function like the region’s Bronze Age temples. Within them, familiar practices and symbols were utilized, but in new ways. This process is hypothesized to reflect new local authorities coalescing their status and power. The integration of cult into daily activities resulted in forms of democratization and inclusivity previously unknown in the region, serving as a link to developments in cultic practices of IA II.

  2. Reassessing Hillforts in Central-Western Iberia: A Geospatial Approach to Hilltop Sites in the Alto Alentejo (Portugal) and Western Extremadura (Spain), Fourth to First Centuries BC
    Fernando Menéndez-Marsh, Tymon C.A De Haas, André Carneiro, Tesse D. Stek · pp. 33-60 · online 2026-08-21
    摘要

    This study proposes a formal geospatial approach to the contexts of fortified hilltop settlements that characterised the Iberian Peninsula during Iron Age (IA) II (fourth to first centuries BC), focusing on an area that encompasses Alto Alentejo in what is today Portugal and western Extremadura in Spain. Although the settlements here represent the most distinctive archaeological sites of this period, they remain poorly understood. The physical inaccessibility of hillforts has limited archaeological studies, while a reliance on Classical sources and ideological narratives of indigenous resistance to Rome promoted by the Iberian dictatorships in the 20th century have led to generic assumptions about the role of IA hillforts in these regions of the Iberian Peninsula. The study highlights their often overlooked diversity, with the goal of challenging generalised interpretations and resolving discrepancies in legacy data. By analysing a selection of hillforts through geospatial modelling, several locational factors can be quantified and used as proxies to associate cultural criteria with each hillfort. This makes it possible to bring out the heterogeneity of the hillforts and to identify two regional patterns in their locational characteristics.
