  3. Stirrup Jars, Coastal Exchange, and Value in the Late Bronze Age Aegean (1470–1075 BC)
    Elliott Fuller · pp. 61-87 · online 2026-08-21
    摘要

    While it is generally assumed that transport stirrup jars functioned within a tightly monitored political economy in the Aegean during the Late Bronze Age, the many jars found outside palatial contexts unsettle this model. To explain the presence of these jars and their role outside the palatial sphere, this article provides a quantitative contextual analysis of transport stirrup jars in the LB II–IIIC period (1420–1075 BC). The data suggest that at least some stirrup jars circulated outside a palatial political economy and that their distribution can be attributed to the agency of merchants. Contrasting patterns of consumption of these jars are also apparent: at palatial sites they were more frequently stockpiled for use in commensal events, while those from non-palatial sites were deposited in ritual contexts. Thus, stirrup jars from palatial sites appear to have been valued for their contents, while those from non-palatial sites were valued as singular possessions. I suggest that this difference can be explained by the way that the meaning of these objects changed as they moved between social realms. Stirrup jars found outside palatial contexts testify to a broader network of exchange involving non-elite actors, as well as to differing sets of values that were actively contested through consumption practices.

  4. New Approaches to Identifying Roman Libraries: Two Late Antique Examples from Stobi (North Macedonia) and Fuente Álamo (Spain)
    Antonio López García, Javier Martínez Jiménez, Eduardo Cerrato Casado · pp. 88-117 · online 2026-08-21
    摘要

    The identification of Roman libraries has long been the subject of debate, with architectural models, written sources and the archaeological record offering seemingly incompatible perspectives. Mamoli, however, has proposed a solution that deploys computational techniques for studying shape grammar to develop library grammar. This is a computational methodology that identifies minimal units of architectural meaning and the ways they may combine to determine if a built space has enough syntactic coherence to be defined securely as a library. In this paper, we use both traditional architectural analysis and the toolkit provided by shape grammar to analyse two Late Antique domestic spaces that have been identified as libraries: Room 7 in the so-called Theodosian Palace of Stobi (North Macedonia), the provincial capital of the province of Macedonia Salutaris; and Room 10 in the villa of Fuente Álamo (Puente Genil, Spain), a Roman site located along a road that connected the coastal cities of the Mediterranean and Corduba, the provincial capital of Baetica. These paired case studies demonstrate the utility of shape grammar as a method for identifying functional spaces in archaeological contexts.

  5. The Pylos Hinterland Revisited: Survey Ceramics and Bottom-Up Approaches to Regional Organization of Late Bronze Age Greece
    Efthymia Tsiolaki · pp. 118-145 · online 2026-08-21
    摘要

    This paper presents a bottom-up perspective on the socioeconomic landscape of Mycenaean Pylos, based on the hitherto untapped potential of legacy survey data. Since the mid-20th century, intensive surface surveys have transformed our understanding of entire regions of the Mediterranean, yet interpretations relying primarily on site-size hierarchies impose a narrow view on the datasets, rendering peripheral sites into passive members of centralized political formations. Using functional analysis of Late Bronze Age ceramics (LH I–LH IIIB2, ca. 1600–1180 BC) collected by the Pylos Regional Archaeological Project (PRAP), this study compares assemblages from both large secondary centers and smaller rural sites within the palatial territory. The results show that communities differed not only in size but also in social status, economic practices, and access to goods, revealing a degree of local agency within the Pylian territory. While similar bottom-up approaches have been applied in other Mediterranean contexts, this study brings such perspectives into the discussion of Mycenaean states, underscoring the value of re-examining legacy survey data to explore regional patterns of social and economic organization.

Advances in Archaeological Practice

Vol. 14, No. 3 · 2026-08 · 17 篇 · Cambridge UP (SAA) · Practice / heritage · Double-blind (verified)
  1. AAP volume 14 issue 3 Cover and Front matter
    pp. f1-f4 · online 2026-09-21
  2. Advances in Archaeological Labor Management
    Allison Mickel · pp. 369-381 · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    Critical scholarship on the state of archaeological labor has boomed and bloomed in recent years, demonstrating systemic harms, inequities, and patterns of exclusion in archaeological work. This body of historical and contemporary analysis has documented low wages, poor working conditions, and efforts to separate the intellectual work from the physical work of archaeology, resulting in diminished recognition and compensation for those performing supposedly “unskilled” labor. Research and reflections on archaeological labor have also demonstrated that such exploitation and marginalization especially affect archaeological workers of marked, nondominant, intersectional identities. With the problems of archaeological labor increasingly clear, this themed issue turns our attention to what can be done to improve working conditions for archaeologists in various sectors and geographic contexts. Recognizing the hierarchies and power structures in the field that position some individuals, groups, and institutions to take effective action to ensure more decent work for all who labor in archaeology, the authors in this issue articulate particular interventions that might be made by the people and institutions most ideally positioned to make change that leads to better labor conditions for all archaeologists and therefore to better archaeology. Here, I outline the robust global scholarship on archaeological labor to situate this themed issue and provide a trail map through the articles gathered here, which aim to ignite sparks for advancing labor management in archaeology.

  3. Toward a Regulation of Archaeological Labor in Spain: Up against a Brick Wall for 40 Years
    Jaime Almansa-Sánchez, F. Rafael Varón-Hernández, Carlos Caballero-Casado, Ángel Astorqui · pp. 382-398 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    Since the first attempts to organize as a collective in the mid-1980s, with the surge of the new management models in a democratic and decentralized Spain, both archaeology and labor have changed considerably. Although many old battles to recognize the profession are still alive, other initiatives have strengthened the archaeological ecosystem in the country. Addressing the structural challenges of work precarity in archaeology is on the horizon. Temporary contracts, low salaries, lack of legal and administrative recognition, and deficiencies in basic rights (for example, in health and safety) are some of the problems we have been facing for decades, and now we are closer to counteracting them. This article will review (1) the recent history of Spanish archaeology and the formation of the current archaeological ecosystem, (2) collective action to improve working conditions in Spanish archaeology over the last decades, (3) the latest actions from the Spanish State Platform of Professional Archaeologists to overcome obstacles, and (4) the dream of a common front in Europe (and beyond) to create the profession we want to have. Although sometimes this struggle is frustrating, a retrospective view of the events and the achievements gained over the years brings hope for the future and some significant steps forward.

  4. In the Shadows of Colonialism: Archaeological Labor Practices in (Post-)Colonial Zimbabwe
    Genius Tevera, Tawanda Mukwende · pp. 399-411 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    Archaeological practice is structured through labor, shaping who participates in fieldwork, who interprets findings, and who produces knowledge. Recent scholarship on archaeological labor in Egypt, Palestine, Turkey, Mexico, Europe, and North America shows that labor regimes are not merely logistical arrangements but constitutive elements of archaeological epistemology, shaping how evidence is generated, interpreted, and circulated. This article examines the historical formation and persistence of archaeological labor practices in Zimbabwe. It traces how colonial archaeology established racialized divisions between African workers and European experts and how these have only been partially reconfigured after independence. Drawing on colonial and postcolonial excavation reports, published scholarship, and recent field practices in Zimbabwe, the analysis shows that postindependence archaeology has not fully dismantled divisions between those who generate field data and those who retain interpretive and authorship authority. The article argues that these labor arrangements reproduce epistemic injustice, limiting whose knowledge counts within archaeological narratives. It proposes a framework for reorganizing archaeological labor through recognition of intellectual labor, inclusive decision-making, transparent authorship and acknowledgment, and embedded capacity building. The article places Zimbabwean archaeology in global debates on labor, expertise, and decolonization, demonstrating that transforming archaeological practice requires reconfiguring labor as a site of knowledge production.

  5. Toward More Inclusive Fieldwork: Labor, Knowledge, and Structural Constraints in Chinese Archaeology
    Wenchao Cai, Jianhui Dong · pp. 412-429 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    Archaeological fieldwork in China commonly relies on short-term labor recruited from nearby villages, forming a long-standing mode of collaboration between state-employed staff and local workers. Drawing on sustained field engagement and interviews at the Chengcun City Site of the Han Dynasty, this research examines how locally recruited workers enter, sustain, and experience participation in archaeological practice. Community members often begin archaeological work during agricultural off-seasons and may accumulate decades of experience. Their trajectories vary: some obtain institutional posts, others return to farming, and many continue in project-based employment. These trajectories indicate that funding structures shape employment continuity and welfare access, while skills developed through long-term practice do not always translate into formal professional status. Archaeological labor constitutes a key mechanism in producing excavation knowledge, shaping professional roles, and structuring heritage governance in everyday practice. Viewed through labor trajectories, archaeological professionalization appears uneven, shaped by institutional arrangements, rural livelihood strategies, and localized opportunities. Based on the Chengcun case, this research points to broader dynamics: large-scale projects rely on localized labor regimes while maintaining differentiated forms of recognition and authority. Reconsidering archaeology through labor offers a perspective on knowledge production, participation, and governance.

  6. From Workers to Companions: Confronting the Contradictions in Neoliberal Social Relationships in Peruvian Archaeology

    摘要

    In the practice of archaeological research in Peru, a set of economic, political, and ethical contradictions can be recognized in the relationships between archaeologists, excavation technicians, and other groups. It has been observed that our social relationships in fieldwork and other archaeological practice spaces reproduce the principles of the free market, individual competition, and labor exploitation. However, a medium-term archaeological project, the Chicama Archaeological Program (PRACH), has been established on the northern coast of Peru, confronting those contradictions to transform socioeconomic relationships in the discipline. Over time, crew members and participants in this program have transformed their ways of being, transcending the conventional boundaries of mere workers. Despite the pervasive influence of the laws of the market on our social fabric, a conscious political stance has emerged as a counterforce, aiming to overcome the alienating effects of neoliberalism. Thus, our crew prioritizes cooperation, solidarity, and care over competition, individualism, selfishness, and immediate and utilitarian relationships. A host of challenges must be confronted, but the motivation for this effort lies in adopting an alternative, novel, and conscious approach to the inequalities generated by conventional forms of interpersonal interaction, especially in our archaeological practice.

  7. Beyond the Trenches: Rethinking Archaeological Labor through Ritual, Relational, and Community Archaeologies in the Central Andean Highlands
    Amanda Brock Morales, Kimberly Munro · pp. 446-468 · online 2026-06-18
    摘要

    Archaeological labor in the Andes unfolds not only in field trenches and labs but also in the social and cosmological landscapes where sites remain powerful actors affecting daily community life. Drawing on collaborative fieldwork in the Huaylas and Cosma River Basins of the Cordillera Negra Mountains, Ancash, Peru, this article reflects on how archaeological work is shaped by theoretical framing, traditional field methods, and the relationships between descendant communities and the archaeological sites they live around that influence daily fieldwork.   8. Working against Silence: Nahua Scholarly Labor, Digital Precarity, and the Politics of Archaeological Sovereignty—The MONARO Project in Veracruz, Mexico

    摘要

  9. Is Archaeological Data Labor Sustainable? Only through Ethical and Policy Reforms, Open Data Emphases on Public Engagement, and Indigenous Data Sovereignty
    Joshua J. Wells, Neha Gupta, Kelsey Noack Myers · pp. 485-507 · online 2026-07-20
    摘要

    The proliferation of information and communication technologies in US archaeology has created myriad expectations for professional labor throughout the life cycle of data. Technological adoption has outpaced the development of professional and ethical frameworks, leaving archaeological data labor unsustainable in our under-resourced discipline. This article reviews traditional Western paradigms underpinning legal and intellectual property models that have shaped archaeological data standards and contribute to problematic outcomes for archaeological data labor, alienate publics from primary data of publicly mandated heritage preservation, disregard Indigenous Data Sovereignty, and enable the extractive commodification of heritage for AI enterprises.   10. Unmanned Aerial Systems and an Archaeology of Accessibility
    Gabriella Armstrong · pp. 508-521 · online 2026-06-18
    摘要

    Archaeology is in the midst of a technological revolution, particularly for data collection, first championed by light detection and ranging (lidar) in the early 2000s and now with widespread applications of unmanned aerial systems (UAS)-deployed remote sensing. While the importance of remote sensing for data collection and heritage conservation is known, less work has acknowledged the implications of remote sensing for field safety, particularly for disabled researchers. UAS offer a unique opportunity to explore circumstances that may affect mobility in the field and how advances in archaeological science can transform fieldwork by improving accessibility for disabled archaeologists. I argue that UAS can—and should—be improved to promote accessibility in archaeological fieldwork, due to their promotion of field safety, increased planning of daily tasks, and broader applications to community-engaged research.   11. Daily Provisions: Tracing a Longue Durée of Feeding Archaeological Workers and Paths of Sustenance for the Future
    Charlotte Williams · pp. 522-539 · online 2026-08-10
    摘要

    Providing sustenance for archaeological workers has been a logistical and social challenge from the earliest stages of the discipline. Food preparation involved a constellation of workers, from gardeners to ingredient purchasers, and from cooks and chefs, who were often teams of locals who catered to and fought against preferences around taste, sustenance, and nutrition for a regionally and internationally diverse group of people. By analyzing food provision lists, material culture, and archival correspondence pertaining to excavations of Coahuila, Mexico (1947) and Piedras Negras, Guatemala (1931–1946), I seek to trace a regional cross section of mid-twentieth-century food preparation for archaeological workers. Food preparation and dining habits are ways in which hierarchical and segregated labor management manifest in archaeology. By juxtaposing these examples with contemporary archaeological sources, I argue that we can radically question the inheritance of quotidian structures of archaeological practice.

  12. Putting the People before the Work: Creating Healthy Working Environments in CRM
    Phyllis S. Johnson · pp. 540-549 · online 2026-06-08
    摘要

    Cultural resource management (CRM) in the United States is an industry that thrives (or falters) on tight budgets and short deadlines. To steadily secure a reliable stream of projects from major clients (and to support the limited budgets of smaller clients), CRM firms are often forced to work with bare-bones funding, requiring them to demand a great deal of effort from their field and office personnel to stay within budget and on schedule. When the projects themselves can easily become the main focus, above the needs of the staff, an unhealthy working environment, lack of work–life balance, burnout, and resentment toward management can result. In this article, I reflect on my own leadership experiences and present a model that outlines best practices for CRM leadership professionals to follow to grow and maintain their businesses through the genuine and consistent support of staff members. I recommend three basic components to creating a healthy working environment in CRM: (1) policies and procedures that clearly outline staff safety, approaches to work–life balance, and field and lab protocols; (2) a “practice what you preach” approach to leadership; and (3) access to professional development opportunities for all staff, regardless of their job level or experience.

  13. Mentoring as Career Infrastructure: Building Sustainable Pathways in Archaeological Practice
    Suanna Selby Crowley · pp. 550-560 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    Sustaining a career in archaeology, particularly one in cultural resources management (CRM), requires more than technical expertise or academic credentialing. Career opportunities have expanded beyond traditional pathways, but the scaffolding that supports professionals across every career stage has not kept pace. Mentoring, in the form of knowledge sharing, advocacy, and intentional networking, can make the difference between leaving the field or thriving in it. This article contends that mentoring is essential career infrastructure, as fundamental to career sustainability as field school training or thesis research. Drawing on experience mentoring more than 75 students, colleagues, and job seekers, this discussion demonstrates how thoughtful mentoring fosters professional development, promotes access, and helps mentees navigate the complex intersections of client work, personal research interests, and public engagement. This article also introduces the concepts of sponsorship—the active promotion of mentees for advancement—and network weaving, the deliberate cultivation of professional connections across broader scales, as critical tools for career readiness, resilience, and inclusion. Case studies illustrate how these practices unlock doors to leadership, expand skill sets, and may help slow attrition. The article concludes with actionable and scalable recommendations for CRM organizations seeking to build the mentoring infrastructure that archaeological careers require.

  14. Building Intentional Leadership and Management Approaches in Archaeology
    Terry Peterkin Brock · pp. 561-568 · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    Archaeologists have a history of borrowing tools, theories, and practices from other disciplines.   15. Fun Labor Is Still Labor: Archaeological Consulting in the Games Industry
    Emma Yasui · pp. 569-577 · online 2026-06-18

  2. Clarifying Stratigraphic Terminology in Paleolithic Archaeology Using Natural Language Processing
    Jovan Galfi, João Cascalheira · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-12
    摘要

    Archaeological context is a foundational concept, yet its definition remains ambiguous and inconsistent. Traditionally, lithostratigraphic frameworks rooted in geology have long dominated, but recent computational and spatial approaches increasingly redefine stratigraphic units. This shift raises a critical challenge: the lack of clear and consistent terminology for defining stratigraphic units. To explore this issue, we applied natural language processing (NLP) to a broad corpus of Paleolithic literature, focusing on the use of key terms such as “layer,” “level,” “occupation,” and “horizon.” Using SciBERT (Scientific BERT; BERT = Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) to generate contextual embeddings, we compared their semantic distributions with a control set of distinct terms (lithic, bone, flake, charcoal). The control set produced stable, separable clusters, whereas contextual terms showed extensive semantic overlap and instability, reflecting their interchangeable and inconsistent use. These results underscore that the problem is not only linguistic but theoretical: without clear terminological baselines, new methodological advances risk conflating analytical tools with interpretive outcomes. We argue that terminological precision is essential for comparability across sites and for cumulative research. NLP offers a replicable framework for auditing and clarifying terminology, contributing to greater rigor and interoperability in archaeological interpretation.

  3. Incorporating Existing Archaeological Collections into Cultural Resource Management Mitigation
    Sarah Herr, Elizabeth Bollwerk, S. Terry Childs, A. Gwynn Henderson, Heather L. Olson, Lori Thompson · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-09-25
    摘要

    Excavation is strongly embedded in cultural resource management (CRM) in the United States as the preferred mitigation of impacts of development, maintenance, or management projects on federal land, funded by federal money, or constructed under federal permits. The law allows more diversity in mitigation than is typically practiced. Consciously integrating the assessment, rehabilitation, research, and interpretation of existing collections of artifacts and associated records into projects conducted in compliance with government regulations is a responsible and public-serving approach to preserving heritage. Case studies from Virginia, New Mexico, Georgia, Rhode Island, and Arizona provide a basis for critically evaluating the challenges and possibilities of this work. Because archaeologists may have less experience reusing existing collections in compliance, the article describes how to assess when such work is an appropriate mitigation and how to identify existing collections, conduct the due diligence involved, and integrate collections work into preservation programs and projects planned within a consultation framework. It also notes that incorporating existing collections can be an effective element of state-, Tribal-, and local-, as well as federal-level compliance. The article concludes by identifying the structures that are in place to support this work and where additional training and resources will be needed.

  4. Navigating the AI Revolution in the Era of Community-Accountable Archaeological Praxis
    Melanie G. Cootsona, Mario Alberto Castillo, Jun Sunseri · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-09-17
    摘要

    This article explores how generative artificial intelligence (AI), particularly Large Language Models (LLMs) such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT, can be mobilized to support collaborative and community-accountable archaeological practice. We situate this exploration within the growing conversation about the use and utility of generative AI in archaeological practice and a discussion about the capabilities of LLMs. Through three case studies—Picuris Pueblo’s legacy collections documentation; the transcription of early twentieth-century archival materials in San Ildefonso Tultepec, Querétaro; and the digitization of the Mountain Messenger —we evaluate the potentials and pitfalls of LLMs in supporting document transcription, organization, and analysis within community-led research contexts. Our findings suggest that, when used transparently and under community direction, LLMs can potentially increase efficiency and accessibility in data processing, expand opportunities for capacity building, and promote citizen-science approaches to heritage stewardship. However, we also identify critical challenges, including data sovereignty, environmental concerns, inequitable access to digital infrastructure, and the persistent need for human oversight to ensure accuracy and accountability.

  5. Integrating Remote Sensing and Indigenous Knowledge: A Community-Commanded Approach to Heritage Management in the Atacama Desert
    Cristian González Rodríguez, Guillermo González Chinga, Valentina Figueroa, Manuel Prieto, Jacinta Arthur, Christian Espíndola et al. · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    This article presents the Zapar project, a community-commanded initiative developed in collaboration with the Atacameño Community of Toconao (northern Chile), which repositions remote sensing (RS) technologies through Indigenous governance and ethical frameworks. By embedding the CARE Principles for Indigenous Data Governance—Collective Benefit, Authority to Control, Responsibility, and Ethics—into every stage of the workflow, the project transforms RS from an extractive documentation tool into a participatory, culturally grounded practice. UAV-based photogrammetry, spatial modeling, and virtual documentation were codesigned with community members to support Indigenous-led heritage management, memory activation, and territorial governance. Beyond technical innovation, this project offers a procedural, epistemological, and ethical-governance advance in archaeological practice. Procedurally, it demonstrates how the CARE Principles can guide RS workflows through shared authority and iterative validation (1) epistemologically, by integrating Indigenous narratives that redefine site interpretation; and (2) ethically, by ensuring data sovereignty and long-term stewardship of digital heritage within the community. The Zapar project therefore provides a replicable framework for decolonizing digital archaeology, demonstrating that technological innovation can coexist with Indigenous leadership, collaborative ethics, and sustainable heritage governance.

  6. Slow, Digital, Immersive, Accessible: A Crip Studies–Centered Framework for Inclusive Archaeological Practice
    Gabriela Oré Menéndez · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    Archaeological practice has historically privileged fieldwork-intensive methodologies that systematically exclude practitioners whose bodies, minds, or life circumstances diverge from disciplinary norms. Drawing on crip theory and intersectional disability studies, this article uses the Homo archaeologus archetype—an often idealized archaeologist characterized by able-bodiedness, neurotypicality, cisgender identity, and physical presence in the field—which functions as both professional stereotype and gatekeeping mechanism. I argue that moving beyond accommodation-based approaches toward accessibility-centered design transforms not only who can participate in archaeology but what the discipline can know. This article proposes a four-pillar framework for inclusive archaeological practice: (1) Slow , restructuring temporal rhythms to prioritize deliberation over efficiency; (2) Digital , integrating computational methods as core archaeology rather than post-fieldwork analysis; (3) Immersive , employing technologies such as photogrammetry and virtual reality (VR) to multiply modes of engagement with archaeological contexts; and (4) Accessible , designing projects from the outset for diverse participation rather than retrofitting accommodations afterward. Through case studies from archaeological projects, I show that these pillars, in one form or another, are already operational in contemporary practice and that recognizing marginalized practitioners as methodological innovators—rather than problems requiring accommodation—strengthens archaeological rigor while expanding epistemic possibilities. This framework reframes accessibility not as an optional enhancement but as a fundamental methodology that benefits all practitioners while centering those historically excluded from disciplinary legitimacy.

  7. Unearthing Indigenous Seed Collections: The State of Ethnobotanical Holdings in US Museums and University Repositories
    Charlotte Dawson, Jewel Cummins, Matthew J. Rowe, Stephanie Russo Carroll, Jane Anderson, Michael Kotutwa Johnson · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    Indigenous communities and scholars are calling for archaeologists and museum professionals to critically assess stewardship of Indigenous agricultural seeds and ethnobotanical collections housed in museums and other institutions and the rematriation of these important cultural items. Indigenous seeds retain cultural and religious significance along with diverse genetic information that could help revitalize Indigenous agriculture systems. Many seeds remain viable, making them especially vulnerable to appropriation and patenting by industrial agriculture corporations searching for climate-resilient seed varieties. We present results of a survey of US museums and universities to determine the prevalence of Indigenous seeds held in collections and to evaluate practices surrounding cataloging, care, storage, and researcher and descendant-community access. Results demonstrate that significant amounts of Indigenous seed stock are currently stored in museums and other institutional repositories, often due to ethnobotanical collecting in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries as well as ongoing archaeological activities from the twentieth century onward. Additionally, based on our findings, we suggest that failure to adequately steward these materials represents a significant risk for further disenfranchisement of Indigenous people from their cultural heritage. We propose pathways to enhance protections of Indigenous seeds and plant materials throughout acquisition and curation processes.

  8. Decolonizing the Spear-Thrower: New Perspectives on Propulsor Projectile Technologies from the Northwest Coast of North America
    Adam N. Rorabaugh, James Brown, Sarah Campbell · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    Historically, much of the archaeological research on spear-throwers has been in the context of comparison with bow and arrow technologies, revealing a colonialist preoccupation with technologies more familiar to Europeans while exoticizing other projectile delivery systems. The commonly used term “atl-atl” from Nahuatl is culturally specific, which at best obscures the existence of multiple regional traditions and independent innovation and development and at worst perpetuates antiquarian exoticism. Other archaeological research, especially experimental work, has focused on understanding the mechanics and physics of using spear-throwers. Although much of this research has had the explicit goal of understanding the functional trade-offs between spear-throwers and bows—and is therefore affected by the implicit colonialist bias—the research also has contributed to a better understanding of spear-thrower design and effectiveness and, most recently, has contributed to reevaluating gender relationships with these technologies. We propose (1) engagement of descendant communities on projectile terminology and (2) a schema that situates propulsor projectiles in the wider context of projectile delivery systems. These steps focus on propulsor technologies as an important dynamic system deserving of its own story in terms of how it was developed, introduced, and refined in local contexts.

  1. The Paradoxes That Create the Space for Treating Archaeological Heritage as a Target in War
    John Carman, Patricia Carman · pp. 207-223 · online 2026-03-30
    摘要

    This article aims to put considerations of threats of damage and destruction to cultural heritage in periods of conflict into a wider context, looking at the nature of war itself as a cultural form. Taking a long-term perspective derived from other work on conflict archaeology, it will start from the premise that a concern for the fate of ‘things’ (however ancient) is a particular pathology of modernity and it is this pathology that creates the conditions under which objects of cultural value are chosen for attention by aggressors and for particular protection by defenders. We shall address war as a realm where violent destruction—of people and places—is the paramount aim, and thereby the equation of people with objects representative of those people becomes an essential part of the discourse of war-making: this in part explains how cultural objects become targets. There is more to war than destruction however: it is an aspect frequently overlooked that wars also create new things—among them new classes of people, new artefacts, new landscapes and new forms of cultural expression. The aftermath of wars also allows things that were destroyed to be remade and re-modelled. As a cultural activity, war is both destructive and creative. Taking this deliberately provocative approach raises important issues about contemporary attitudes to the destruction of heritage and its protection in wartime.

  1. Antiracism in Online Archaeology Teaching: An Effort in Educational Reckoning
    Molly Morgan, Kesia Knowles · online 2026-08-19
    摘要

    Drawing from a collaborative teaching and design partnership, the authors present Knowing the Past , a four-course online specialization on the Coursera platform designed to introduce archaeology through an antiracist lens. By integrating decolonial content and antiracist teaching practices in an online open global learning environment, the case study offers a model for how universities can take concrete, reparative steps through what and how they teach. The example also integrates antiracist theory into course design, through carefully chosen content and structured activities. This teaching approach illustrates how instructional design can extend STEM educational reckoning into global learning spaces when approached as a form of ethical praxis.

  1. Distribution and Cultural Characteristics of Bronze Age Sites in the Mangyeong River Basin
    Jongcheol Lee · pp. 6-38 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    만경강 유역권의 청동기시대 연구는 익산의 청동기문화 연구에서 비롯되었다. 그리고 전북혁신도시 일원의 발굴조사를 계기로 독자적인 고고학의 영역으로 전환될 수 있었다. 이러한 배경을 바탕으로 북쪽의 미륵산에서 남쪽의 모악산 사이에 펼쳐져 있는 분지형 공간을 ‘만경강 유역 문화권’으로 설정할 수 있게 된 것은 고고학적으로 의미가 작지 않다.만경강은 전북 지역을 남과 북으로 가르며, 여러 지류들은 세분된 지역권을 만들어준다. 상류권인 동쪽의 고산권을 비롯하여, 북쪽은 봉동권·왕궁권·익산권·서수-황등권·군산권으로, 남쪽은 용진권·건지산권·황방산권· 백구권·김제권으로 구분할 수 있다. 북쪽은 익산권에, 남쪽은 황방산권과 건지산권에 유적이 집중적으로 분포하는 특징을 보인다.이러한 유적의 분포를 배경으로 청동기시대는 전기-중기-후기의 체계로 구분된다. 전기에는 늦은 시기의 미사리유형·가락동유형 및 흔암리유형 단계 유적의 존재, 익산권에 전기∼중기 단계 어느 시점에 요령식동검문화의 존재를 살필 수 있다. 중기에는 황방산권이 중추적인 문화중심지로 성장, 거점취락의 존재와 지역연계망의 구축, 지석묘와 송국리형 묘제의 다양한 조합, 체계적인 취락설계와 사회적 위계체계 형성 등을 추출할 수 있다. 후기에는 소수의 유적을 통해 대규모이거나 체계적인 취락의 보편성 부재를 엿볼 수 있다.한편 중상위 규모/체제의 취락들에서는 집체적 성격의 제의행위가가 존재하였다. 전기에는 환구와 수혈을, 중기에는 광장에서 수혈이나 입대목(立大木)을 활용하였다. 이러한 문화 전통은 후기 단계 취락으로 전승되는데, 문화 집단 간 복합관계를 설명해주는 근거가 된다.

  2. The Current Status and Characteristics of Mound- Shaped Embankments in the Yeongsan River Basin
    Okmin Han · pp. 39-67 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    본고는 영산강유역에서 확인되는 이른바 ‘빈무덤’ 현상의 유구 사례를 대상으로 그 기능과 성격을 규명하는 데 목적이 있다. 이를 위해 매장 목적의 고분과 구별되는 비고분적 특징에 주목하였으며, 해당 사례를 ‘분구상성토물(墳 丘狀盛土物)’로 개념화하여 검토하였다.검토 결과, 분구상성토물 사례는 담양에서 해남에 이르는 영산강유역 곳곳에서 확인된다. 주요 특징으로는 중심매장시설의 부재를 비롯하여 다장을 전제하지 않는 고정형 분구, 구릉 능선상의 입지, 독립적인 공간 점유, 주구시설의 미비, 그리고 입대목·입석·계단시설 등 특정 경관시설의 수반을 들 수 있다. 또한 이들 상당수는 고분군 내에 병존하면서 형태·기술·상징성을 공유하고 있으며, 기능적으로는 매장보다 의례·기념적 성격을 보다 강하게 드러낸다. 공간적으로는 해양과 내륙을 잇는 주요 교통로상의 고개마루나 취락의 초입부에 독립적으로 조성되거나, 묘역 내 특정 지점에 배치됨으로써 고분과 차별화되는 독자적인 기능을 수행한 것으로 판단된다.분구상성토물은 분구 자체에 상징성이 부여된 고총고분과 동일한 사회적 맥락 속에서 그 축조 배경을 찾을 수 있으며, 지역사회의 권력 구조와 집단의 정체성을 가시적으로 완성시킨 의례·기념물로 기능하였다. 특히 동시기 신라·가야·일본열도 등 여타 지역에서는 찾을 수 없는 마한 고유의 정신문화적 산물로서, 마한인들의 관념적 기반과 의례체계를 복원할 수 있는 중요한 고고자료로 평가된다. 나아가 이러한 전통이 공주 송산리고분군 능역에서도 확인된다는 사실은 백제가 마한의 정신문화를 배척하지 않고 일정 부분 수용·계승하였음을 재확인시켜주는 자료라는 점에서 의의가 크다.향후에는 개별 유구의 축조 맥락과 행위 흔적에 대한 정밀한 분석을 토대로 고분과 구별되는 의례 공간으로서의 위상을 보다 구체화할 필요가 있다. 그동안 분구상성토물이 충분히 조명되지 못한 배경에는 물질자료 중심의 해석경향과 기존의 관성적인 인식 틀이 일정 부분 작용했던 것으로 보인다. 앞으로의 연구에서는 이를 신중하게 검토하여 유사한 해석상의 오류가 반복되지 않기를 기대한다.

  3. The Distribution and Significance of Wa-Type Archaeological Remains in the Hanseong Period Baekje Region
    Dongsun Oh · pp. 69-97 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    한성기 백제권에서 출토된 왜계 고고자료가 시기별로 어떤 분포상의 특징을 보이는지, 그리고 그 의미가 무엇인지를 고분 부장유물을 중심으로 살펴보았다.왜계 고고자료는 4세기 말~5세기 초부터 무기류를 중심으로 서남해안 지역에 유입되기 시작한다. 5세기 중반부터는 왜계 토기가 급격하게 유입된다. 백제와 왜의 교섭이 활성화되면서 토착 세력과 외부 세력의 접촉면이 넓어지는 양상을 보이며, 특히 영산강 유역권에서 왜계 토기의 밀도가 압도적으로 높게 나타난다. 이는 이 일대가 왜와의 교류에 특화된 지역이었을 가능성을 시사한다. 아울러 그 배후에는 백제의 정치적 의도도 작용하고 있었던 것으로 보인다. 한편 중부 내륙의 천안 지역에는 서남해안과 유사한 구조의 왜계 고분이 조영된다. 당시 교통로는 왜계 고고자료의 분포양상으로 보아, 낙동강 하구를 기점으로 남원 아영 분지를 거쳐 북상하는 육로가 있었던 것으로 추정된다. 이와 함께 연안 항로와 육로를 연계하는 방식도 활용되었다. 도성으로 진입하기 위한 중부 내륙의 거점지역인 천안·연기·청주일대에는 왜계 무기류의 밀도가 높고, 왜계 고분도 조영되는 것으로 보아 백제 중앙을 방비하기 위한 목적으로서 왜인의 활동 가능성을 상정할 수 있다.결론적으로 한성기 백제는 왜와의 교류 과정에서 교통로를 다변화하고, 점차 토착 세력과의 접촉면을 넓혀 가면서 영향력을 확대해 나가고 있었다. 이 과정에서 영산강 유역권뿐만 아니라 도성에 인접한 내륙 거점지역에서도 왜인을 군사적 목적으로 이용한 것으로 판단된다.

  4. A Reexamination of the Evolution of Burial Mounds and Tomb-Building Groups on the Haenam Peninsula (3rd-6th Centuries)
    Sangjin Jeon · pp. 100-133 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    본 연구는 3세기부터 6세기에 이르는 해남반도 고분의 변천과정을 재검토하고, 이를 바탕으로 고분 축조세력과 왜계고분의 성격을 재검토하는 데 목적이 있다. 기존 연구에서는 5세기를 기점으로 백포만권역에서 도암만권역으로 중심지가 이동한 것으로 인식되어 왔다. 이러한 해석은 5세기에 도암만권역에서 왜계요소를 포함한 고분이 집중적으로 등장하고 백포만권역 고분이 상대적으로 감소하는 현상에 근거한 것이었다. 그러나 최근 발굴조사 성과를 반영할 경우, 이러한 변화는 단순한 중심지 이동으로 설명하기 어려워 보인다.이에 본 연구에서는 해남반도를 4개의 권역으로 구분한 후, 각 권역별 고분의 입지·분형·매장시설을 검토하고 부장토기를 근거로 편년을 설정하였다. 그 결과 해남반도 고분은 제형 분구와 토광이 중심을 이루는 초기 단계에서 원형분구와 석곽이 등장하는 단계, 그리고 횡혈식석실과 전방후원형 분구가 나타나는 단계로 변화하는 양상을 확인하였다. 또한 이러한 변화는 부장토기의 편년과도 대체로 일치하며, 고분의 분포와 전개 양상이 권역별로 차이를 보인다는 점도 파악하였다.따라서 해남반도 고분문화는 특정 권역에서 일방적으로 확산된 것이 아니라, 각 권역 재지세력의 지속과 변동 속에서 전개된 것으로 이해할 수 있다. 특히 5세기 이후 나타나는 왜계요소는 외래집단이 직접 이주한 결과라기보다 재지세력의 선택적 수용과 해상교류를 기반으로 한 집단의 활동이 복합적으로 반영된 결과로 볼 수 있다. 따라서 해남반도 고분문화의 전개는 중심지 이동의 관점이 아니라, 권역별 발전 과정과 외래요소 수용 방식의 차이라는 관점에서 설명하는 것이 타당하다.

  5. Change and Significance of Three Kingdoms Period Pottery in the Goheung Region
    Hyunju Seo, Haneul Oh · pp. 134-168 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    본고에서는 최근 조사된 동호덕고분, 장덕리 산81번지유적 토기가마 출토 토기를 포함시켜 고흥지역 삼국시대토기의 변천 양상을 북부와 남부로 나누어 살펴본 후, 이를 해남, 장흥 등 인근지역과 비교하면서 고분과 함께 토기변천에 반영된 의미를 파악해 보았다.고흥지역의 삼국시대 토기는 가야, 왜, 영산강유역 토기와 비교하거나 사비기의 고분 연구성과를 포함시켜 10기(4세기 후엽~7세기 중엽)로 구분하였다. 북부지역은 4세기 후엽부터 김해지역 등의 금관가야계 토기가 보이다가 5 세기 대에는 진주지역 등과 관련되며 주로 소가야계 토기가 나타난다. 이와 달리 남부지역은 4세기 후엽에 가야계토기가 나타나 5세기 대까지 이어지는데 이는 주로 함안, 창원, 김해 등의 낙동강중·하류지역과 관련된다. 5세기중엽에는 창녕지역 토기도 추가된다. 인근지역 중 고흥 남부지역과 비슷한 토기 양상을 보이는 곳은 해남 현산면 일대이며, 장흥지역은 고흥 남부와 북부의 양상이 혼재된 모습이다. 고흥 남부지역에서는 5세기 전엽부터 왜계 자료도 나타나고, 5세기 후엽부터 백제 기종이 늘어나는데 웅진기 후반에는 왜계, 가야계와 신라계 토기가 반입뿐 아니라 모방도 이루어지고, 지역형식의 개배도 나타난다. 일부 외래계 토기 기종은 사비기 고분 출토 토기에 이어진다.고흥지역은 토기와 고분의 변천 양상으로 보아 4세기 후엽부터 가야계 토기와 고분이 나타나 5세기 전·중엽까지 이어지고, 남부지역에는 왜계고분도 공존하였다. 이로 보아 고흥 남부와 북부에는 서로 다른 재지세력이 존재했고, 백제 근초고왕 남정 후 가야와 적극적으로 교류하면서 해상세력으로 성장한 것으로 추정된다. 5세기 중엽 이후북부와 남부에서 영산강유역을 포함한 백제 토기가 늘어나고 토기 생산도 이루어지기 시작한 것은 안동고분의 백제계 위세품 사여와 관련될 것이다. 안동고분의 피장자는 왜인일 가능성이 있으며 한성기 백제의 군사적 활동 지원을계기로 들어와 고흥 남부지역에 정착한 것으로 추정된다. 웅진기에도 고흥 남부지역에는 고총 조영이 이어지는데, 6세기 중엽 이른시기에 조영된 동호덕고분이 그 양상을 잘 보여준다. 이 고분은 가야계 분구와 석실묘, 호석(추정), 왜계 즙석, 다양한 외래계 유물(반입, 모방 토기 등)과 함께 백제의 토기, 관고리 목관 사용 등의 매장관습도 확인된다. 이 고분의 다양한 외래계 요소는 5세기를 전후한 시기에 낙동강중·하류지역의 가야 문물을 적극 수용하며 성장했던 고흥 남부의 재지세력이 왜인집단의 정착으로 혼재하면서, 이전 시기에 이어 신라화되어가는 가야지역과 교류를 지속한 결과로 추정된다. 동호덕고분의 규모, 조영 시기로 보아 고흥 남부의 재지세력은 백제의 섬진강유역 진출후 백제 해상세력으로써 군사적 활동을 하였고, 인근 사비기 고분과 토기에도 일부 요소가 이어지는 점에서 사비기방군성제도 하에서도 그러한 활동을 이어나갔던 것으로 추정된다.

  6. A Study of Funerary Practices of 5th and 6th Centuries Tombs in the Yeongsan River Basin
    HYO BEEN KIM · pp. 170-198 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    이 글은 영산강유역 5~6세기 고분을 대상으로 장송의례 전개 과정을 복원하는 데 목적이 있다. 이를 위해 고분의주요 공간을 정지층과 기반층, 묘광과 매장시설, 분구 성토층, 분구 위, 주구로 나누고 각 장소에서 나타나는 의례흔적을 종합적으로 분석하였다.영산강유역 장송의례는 고분 축조 단계와 연동되며 각 장소로 구분되는 의례를 수행하였다. 정지층과 기반층에서는 기물을 매개로 지신을 향한 의례가 수행되었다. 매장시설에서는 기물 매납, 주칠, 음식물 공헌 등을 통해 피장자의 승천과 안녕을 염원하는 의례가 이루어졌다. 분구 성토층에서는 폐쇄의례, 고분 완성, 고분의 안정을 기원하는 의례가 행해졌다. 분구 위와 주구에서는 기물 훼기, 동물 희생 등 죽음과 현세를 분리하는 의례가 이루어졌다.토광묘, 목관(곽)묘, 옹관묘, 석곽묘는 정지층과 기반층 의례 → 매장시설 축조 의례 → 시신 안치와 기물 부장 → 안치시설 외부에서 의례 → 분구 성토층 의례 → 주구 의례로 이어지는 일련의 흐름을 갖는다. 반면, 석실묘는 축조후 일정 시간이 지나고 납관이 이루어지는 흐름으로 인해 분구 성토층과 매장시설 축조 의례 → 시신 안치와 기물부장 의례 → 폐쇄의례→ 성토층 의례 → 분구 위 의례, 주구 의례의 과정을 가진다. 주구에서의 의례는 고분 완성 이후에 국한되지 않고 고분을 축조하는 여러 시점과 단계에서 의례가 수행된 것으로 추정된다. 영산강유역의 장송의례는 고분의 장소와 묘제별로 독립적으로 수행되면서도 전반적으로 축조-안치-폐쇄-폐기라는 절차에서 수행되었다.매장시설 외부에 뚜껑과 토기를 함께 매납하는 외부 부장 의례가 영산강유역 모든 묘제에서 공통적으로 나타난다. 이는 묘제의 변화 속에서 현지 세력의 고유한 장송 관념이 지속되었음을 알 수 있다. 시각적으로 피장자가 보이지 않거나 매장시설을 최종적으로 덮기 직전의 위치에서 토기가 확인되는 것으로 보아 매장시설의 마지막 단계에 수행된 의례로 이해된다. 주로 석재나 토기로 막음한 상태로 출토되며, 내부에 음식물을 담았을 것으로 생각된다.따라서 영산강유역의 매장시설에서 피장자를 향한 마지막 의례로 음식물 공헌이 이루어졌던 것으로 추정된다.이러한 연구 결과는 마한사회 유적의 복원에서 유물과 유구의 재현을 넘어, 장송의례를 통해 정신문화와 의미까지 복원할 때 비로소 완성됨을 설명하는 것이다. 이는 고고자료에서 물질이 과거의 행위를 드러내는 한편, 의례는 그 의미와 가치를 해석하는 핵심 단서로 기능하기 때문이다.

호서고고학 (Journal of the Hoseo Archaeological Society)

Vol. 64 · 2026-06-30 · 8 篇 · 호서고고학회 · 한국고고학 · Peer-reviewed (double-blind not verified)
  1. An Archaeological Understanding of (Prehistoric) Social Structure
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Bumcheol Kim · pp. 4-23 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    The concept of social structure has long occupied a central position in archaeological interpretations of past societies. Since the early twentieth century, when structural-functionalism provided a dominant theoretical framework, archaeology has increasingly incorporated perspectives from modern sociology that emphasize the role of social actors, thereby enabling more dynamic explanations of social change in the past. Meanwhile, archaeology has engaged with significant methodological and theoretical issues relevant to proper understanding of diachronic/synchronic variation of social structures. These include efforts to identify social complexity through material remains, to account for inconsistencies between social principles reconstructed from different lines of archaeological evidence, and to adopt multi-scalar approaches to social transformation. In Korean archaeology, such developments have been actively employed to interpret the rapidly growing body of archaeological data since the 2000s. Nevertheless, it remains questionable whether these concepts have been consistently and rigorously applied, and whether the social structures of Korean prehistory have been sufficiently theorized in a productive and systematic manner.

  2. A Preliminary Study on Reconstructing the Social Dynamics of Paleolithic and Neolithic Societies in Korea
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Minkoo Kim · pp. 24-47 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This paper focuses on the dynamics between factors that generate hierarchy among members of society and egalitarian mechanisms that suppress such hierarchy, in order to supplement the perspective that dichotomizes prehistoric societies into egalitarian and inegalitarian ones. To this end, it examines the limitations of neo-evolutionary classifications and considers the processes through which hierarchy emerges, as well as the mechanisms by which it is controlled by the majority of social members, through examples from great apes and hunter-gatherer societies. Social hierarchization and counter-hierarchization interact with various factors, including population growth, surplus production, exchange, communal labor, and intergroup conflict, and their specific manifestations vary according to the social and environmental conditions faced by a given group. Therefore, the Paleolithic and Neolithic periods cannot be defined as consistently egalitarian or inegalitarian societies. An analysis of the Korean Neolithic period, particularly the Unseo-dong site in Incheon, shows that although some signs of hierarchization appeared in large-scale Neolithic settlements, the development into a hierarchical society was ultimately limited by restraining mechanisms such as group fission. Prehistoric societies on the Korean Peninsula should therefore be understood not as having a fixed character, but as fluid and complex structures in which the power of a minority oriented toward hierarchy coexisted with the power of the majority seeking to maintain equality.

  3. Various Issues in the Discussion on the Emergence of Regional Social Organizations in the Bronze Age
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Dongseok Kang · pp. 48-73 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This paper examines the problems and limitations of theories regarding the emergence of large-scale social organizations during the Bronze Age and proposes several approaches to overcome them. Previous studies have sought to reconstruct large-scale social organizations —in which social complexity began to increase— based on evidence such as the restructuring of the Bronze Age economy around agriculture, the emergence of large-scale settlements, wealth inequality, and the circulation of prestige goods. This was primarily explained as a fixed, unidirectional, vertical settlement system. However, this approach had the limitation of failing to comprehensively address the multifaceted patterns of emergence of social organizations that would have unfolded dynamically during the process of social complexity. To overcome this, three approaches were proposed. First, an approach based on the heterarchy perspective is necessary. The heterarchy approach, which defines settlement relationships through a complex system where hierarchical relationships and horizontal interactions intersect, can explain the diverse patterns of emergence of social organizations. Second, it is necessary to develop the discussion of social organization from the perspective of strategies for expanding political power. Leaders who emerge during the process of social complexity attempt to expand political power through the control of labor, the distribution of goods, and the manipulation of ideology; these actions serve as both the source of power and the backdrop for the formation of social organizations. The relationship between these leaders’ power expansion strategies and the formation of social organizations can be explained through the settlement density in the Hoseo region and the distribution networks of prestige goods in the Namgang River basin. Third, we need a multi-scalar approach that moves beyond the limitation of viewing settlements as fixed entities and instead understands them as multi-layered spatial relationships that overlap, combine, and interconnect across various scales. By reconstructing settlement patterns from a dialectical socio-spatial perspective, we can provide a more comprehensive explanation of the emergence of early complex social organizations.

  4. Decline and Resilience of the Songguk-ri Culture: future research prospects
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Heejin Lee · pp. 74-94 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This paper reviews an approach to interpreting social change from an ecological perspective and reviews previous studies on the correlation between social continuity and environmental change during the Bronze Age. Furthermore, it explores key considerations for advancing future research on the prosperity and decline of Bronze Age culture. Although previous studies regarding the climate of the Korean Bronze Age are limited, they can be summarized as follows: The impact of cooling trend on social structure has been discussed in connection with the onset of the Bronze Age, the development of agriculture during the Early Bronze Age, the expansion of arable land and rice cultivation due to alluvial developments, and the decline of the Songguk-ri culture. Additionally, pilot studies have applied models such as the Panarchy Adaptive Cycle to understand climate change not merely as a physical phenomenon but within a socio-economic context. Meanwhile, this paper examines the necessary steps to further research social change in the Bronze Age from an ecological perspective. It emphasizes that, alongside efforts to specify the actual ripple effects of climate change, a prior understanding of the complexity of Bronze Age social systems —which determines human response mechanisms to external variables— is essential. Specifically, this paper suggests the need for a review of the functioning of the overall economic system (including the production, distribution, and consumption of economic surplus) and the formation and operation of cultural resilience as observed in rituals and other crisis-response mechanisms.

  5. A New Approach to Understanding Social Structure in Prehistoric Korea through Dietary Analysis: Stable Isotope Evidence from Human Remains
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Kyungcheol Choy, Juyoung Park · pp. 95-126 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    Recent studies of Prehistoric social structures have suggested new interpretive possibilities through bioarchaeological approaches. Bioarchaeological methods are used to infer social relationships such as social status, gendered divisions of labor, and hierarchical organization by examining differences in health conditions and labor intensity reflected in human skeletal remains. Accordingly, bioarchaeology is a useful tool for understanding prehistoric social structures and social differentiation by linking individual life histories with their broader social contexts. This study applies stable isotope analysis to human remains from archaeological sites to investigate dietary patterns and social classification in Prehistoric Korea. By examining differences in dietary patterns according to sex, age, and individuals, this research explores social differentiation reflected in dietary practices. Furthermore, the results of this analysis are considered alongside previous studies that have examined social structures through the multidimensional analysis of factors such as human remains, burial structures, and associated grave artifacts. By combining the analysis of dietary patterns with burial systems, this study aims to provide a more comprehensive understanding of patterns of social differentiation in prehistoric societies. Stable isotopic studies on dietary patterns suggest that there was no clear social stratification based on sex or age during the Neolithic and Bronze Ages. Distinct dietary differences associated with social status or gender become more clearly visible only during the Three Kingdom period. In contrast, researches on burial practices indicate that the formation of social hierarchies and increasing horizontal segmentation within communities began as early as the Bronze Age. This discrepancy between the two approaches to social differentiation may suggest that the social hierarchies expressed through mortuary practices were not directly reflected in the daily dietary practices of individuals. In other words, dietary patterns tend to be relatively stable and conservative. Even when drastic changes occur in subsistence economies or social stratification, these shifts are not immediately reflected in diet; rather, clear dietary differences tend to emerge only when social stratification and hierarchical differentiation become more pronounced and well-established.

  6. Core Sites and Linkage Strategies of the Jungwon Historical and Cultural Zone: A Case Study of Chungju City
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Sieun Yang, Shinyeong Park · pp. 127-156 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This study aims to re-examine the characteristics of major archaeological and cultural heritage sites in Chungju, the core area of the Jungwon Historical and Cultural Zone, and to propose linkage strategies for systematic conservation, management, and utilization at the regional scale. This discussion is situated within the context of the enforcement of the Special Act on the Maintenance of Historical and Cultural Zones. The Jungwon Historical and Cultural Zone is not the cultural sphere of a single political entity, but a integrative cultural space in which multiple historical and cultural layers from Mahan to Unified Silla overlapped through the Namhan River waterway and inland transportation networks. However, the zone still suffers from low public recognition and an abstractly perceived identity. Therefore, identifying core sites that can concretely demonstrate its historical value and establishing linkage strategies among these sites are essential tasks. This study identifies three core zones in Chungju: the Jungangtap-myeon area, the Tangeumdae area, and the Chungju-eupseong area. The Jungangtap-myeon area, where numerous sites from the Tappyeong-ri settlement to the Tappyeong-ri Seven-story Stone Pagoda are concentrated, clearly demonstrates the layered historical and cultural strata of Baekje, Goguryeo, Silla, and Unified Silla. The Tangeumdae area, centered on the Chilgeum-dong iron production site and Tangeumdae earthen fortress, represents a space where iron production, the Namhan River waterway, and Baekje’s local governance intersected. The Chungju-eupseong area illustrates the historical continuity from the ancient administrative center of Jungwon-gyeong to the medieval and early modern historical city of Chungju. To organically connect these three zones, this study proposes three major strategies: integrated management based on the Historic Urban Landscape approach, strengthening the identity and public recognition of the Jungwon Historical and Cultural Zone, and establishing community-driven heritage utilization models. This approach moves beyond the preservation of individual heritage sites and seeks to restore a regional historical and cultural environment in which heritage, space, and people interact. Ultimately, it can contribute to building a sustainable, citizen-centered model for the conservation, management, and utilization of historical and cultural zones.

  7. A Web-Based Archaeological Terrain Analysis System and AI-Assisted Report Writing Methodology
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Uihaeng Heo, Khujin Oh · pp. 157-183 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    This study presents the development background, process, and methodological application of ArchaeoGIS Pro, an archaeology-specific geographic information system (GIS) that operates entirely within a web browser without requiring dedicated software installation or server infrastructure. Current archaeological GIS practice faces three representative challenges: dependence on expensive commercial software, insufficient reflection of Korea-specific archaeological contexts, and a persistent gap between spatial analysis outputs and written report narratives. To address these issues, a terrain analysis system named ArchaeoGIS Pro was developed. The system derives archaeologically meaningful terrain information from digital elevation models (DEMs)—including slope, aspect, hillshade, curvature, and the Topographic Wetness Index (TWI)—and incorporates a criteria-based exploratory overlay analysis module for assessing site suitability, along with spatial analysis functionality. The analytical results are integrated with a large language model (LLM) to explore the feasibility of generating draft natural environment descriptions conforming to the format of Korean buried cultural heritage excavation reports. Numerical comparison with established commercial GIS software confirmed high agreement across major analytical parameters. The system can be accessed and tested at https://www.archaeology.kr/Archaeo_GIS.html, allowing independent verification of the analytical functions described in this paper. The LLM-assisted report drafting function represents a preliminary attempt to bridge the gap between GIS analysis outputs and written archaeological narratives. At this stage, objective evaluation criteria for assessing the quality of AI-generated drafts have not yet been fully established, and systematic comparison with actual excavation reports is identified as a future research objective. The study also acknowledges limitations including the lack of sufficient field application cases, the need for validation of site suitability criteria across different periods and site types, and the requirement for archaeology-specific AI training data. Despite these limitations, the system demonstrates the potential of integrating web-based GIS terrain analysis with AI-assisted reporting as a practical auxiliary tool for archaeological research.

  8. Issues and Improvement Measures Concerning the Buried Heritage System under the National Heritage Impact Assessment Act
    The hoseo Archaeological Society, Doheon Kim · pp. 184-211 · online 2026-06-30
    摘要

    With the recent introduction of the national heritage impact assessment system, impact assessments for buried heritage are now being conducted. Although the national heritage impact assessment system has many administrative advantages in that it integrates several pre-existing systems, the provisions concerning buried heritage have largely retained the problems of the previous system. Moreover, there has thus far been little substantive discussion of the buried heritage system itself. Based on this awareness of the problem, this study examines the issues inherent in the buried heritage system. The review suggests that the buried heritage system has the following problems. First, although the law formally advocates preventive investigation, prediction, and assessment, the subordinate regulations in practice operate only within limited parameters, focusing on areas presumed to contain buried heritage, previously investigated areas, projects of 30,000 square meters or more, and areas publicly designated by local governments. In addition, although surface surveys occupy an important position within the buried heritage system, there are insufficient mechanisms to compensate for their methodological limitations. In other words, the current system risks being reduced to one that manages only areas that are already known or that meet certain formal criteria, rather than one that broadly investigates areas where buried heritage may potentially exist. To address these problems, it is first necessary to strengthen investigations of land surrounding areas presumed to contain buried heritage. In addition, alongside zones publicly designated by the heads of local governments, surface surveys should also be permitted, as in the past, when the head of a local government deems them necessary. This study also argues that monitoring surveys should be excluded from preservation measures and that such surveys should instead be made mandatory when construction is carried out after a surface survey. Furthermore, it would be appropriate to operate an organization such as a committee to review surface survey reports and related materials; once this system is firmly established, cases in which authority is delegated to local governments could also be eliminated. Finally, from a long-term perspective, this study argues that it would be desirable to adopt investigation methods that involve actual excavation to verify buried remains, such as sample surveys or trial excavations, rather than relying on surface surveys.

  1. A monumental apsidal building of the Roman Imperial period in Doliche
    Michael Blömer, Lennart Wouter Kruijer, Theresa Pommer · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-06-15
    摘要

    Excavations in the city center of the ancient city of Doliche in southeast Türkiye have brought to light the remains of a monumental apsidal building. Although the structure is poorly preserved due to looting and spoliation, the results of three seasons of excavation allow for an initial analysis and preliminary reconstruction. This article presents the building for the first time and discusses its importance in improving our understanding of monumental urban architecture in the region. The building is interpreted as a temple, and the article explores its potential links with apsidal temples in southern Syria and the Roman imperial cult.

  4. Origins and features of Early Roman priesthoods: literary sources, images and material culture
    Francesco De Stefano, Francesca Fulminante, Jörg Rüpke · pp. 28-75 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    This paper examines three early Roman priesthoods, specifically, the Vestal virgins, the Salii, and the Flamines, through the lens of their visual and symbolic prerogatives, adopting a diachronic perspective based on literary sources, iconographic evidence, and archaeological data. The study highlights the deep historical roots of priestly paraphernalia in pre- and proto-historic funerary contexts, as well as a significant shift in gender dynamics. Prior to the 8th c. BCE, numerous male religious and martial figures are depicted with double shields and other distinctive attributes. From the 8th c. BCE to the Archaic period, both male and female religious figures appear, often equipped with knives, while double shields gradually disappear. This pattern suggests an expanding definition of religious and political prerogatives. By the 8th c. BCE, specialized male and female figures (identified by their knives) assume religious roles, whereas male warriors become kings ( reges ), holding political authority and often serving as Augurs as well. *

  5. Situating a rock-born god: place, practice, and geologies of Mithras-worship at Močići (Croatia)
    Ian S. Wilson, Matthew McCarty · pp. 76-104 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    Contemporary accounts of Mithraism emphasize particular, shared modes of anthropogenic place-making: spaces laid out in distinctive, bench-lined arrangements, which may have been freighted with a surplus of cosmic symbolism that was actualized through the performance of ritual. Focusing on the site of Močići (Croatia), we argue that Mithraic place-making must instead be situated in a host of far more localized and material relationships that afford their own particular modes, experiences, and semiotics of worshipping the god. In the case of Močići, those relationships include local karstic geologies, particular lifeways and experiences of the landscape, and the ways that significances were woven around natural phenomena. The site encourages rethinking both contemporary conceptions of “Mithraism” and material- and place-based approaches to cult in antiquity.

  6. Patronage and public amenities in Roman Epirus: the well of Junia Rufina at Butrint
    Milena Melfi · pp. 105-129 · online 2026-06-19
    摘要

    The well of Junia Rufina has long been one of the main features of the archaeology of Roman Butrint. This paper aims at offering a chronological and cultural context for its dedication by inserting it into contemporary urban and historical developments. The monumentalization of the well in the 2nd c. CE illustrates how increased access to and architectural display of water was a main aspect of urban policy in the newly founded province of Epirus, in line with the rest of the Greek world. The role of Rufina, on the other hand, can be understood in the light of the cultural policies of the time, and as a manifestation of the power and prestige attached to female patronage in highly networked families throughout the Empire at the time of Hadrian.

  7. The supply of metals in the later Roman Empire: framing the question
    W. V. Harris · pp. 130-167 · online 2026-06-15
    摘要

    Whether the later Roman Empire ran short of precious or base metals, either at the level of the state or in private life, is a question that can only be answered in a tentative fashion. Surveying the period from the mid-2nd to the mid-7th c., this article weighs the difficulties presented by the full range of material and textual evidence, while attempting to establish a balance between all regions of the Roman world. It concludes by suggesting that there probably were indeed crises of metal production at certain periods, caused by the depletion of usable ores and hypothetically by a shortage of fuel.

  8. Changing patterns of fineware consumption in Roman Mediterranean Gaul, ca. 225 BCE–100 CE
    Benjamin Luley · pp. 168-201 · online 2026-04-10
    摘要

    Widely found at archaeological sites across the Roman Empire, the appearance in the late 1st c. BCE onward of the red gloss ceramic referred to as terra sigillata signals important transformations in the socio-economic organization of production and consumption for provincial societies. Nonetheless, relatively few studies have explored diachronically the ways in which the appearance of terra sigillata may have impacted local lifeways compared with the uses of earlier ceramics. This article explores these issues in the context of Roman Mediterranean Gaul, focusing in particular on the region of eastern Languedoc, by comparing, in both discard and funerary contexts, the differential uses of black gloss ceramics from the 3rd to the 1st c. BCE with later terra sigillata vessels. The evidence discussed here suggests that the appearance of terra sigillata was important in reifying more individual-centered social relationships in dining and other aspects of daily life.

  9. Not just a housekeeper: a new look at the work of the Roman vilica
    Tamara Lewit · pp. 202-238 · online 2026-07-13
    摘要

    The presence of the vilica or female supervisor at a Roman villa is attested across five centuries in varied texts, and her duties are detailed in an entire book of Columella’s On Agriculture . This paper challenges assumptions by modern scholars that her managerial functions were confined to those of a housekeeper, focused on food supplies for the household and the supervision of domestic labor inside the house. Through closer examination of textual, iconographic, and archaeological evidence, we can see that the vilica ’s principal role was to oversee not the domestic sphere, but rather a range of vital productive activities on the farm. In particular, she seems to have been responsible for wine and oil making and for important rituals linked to production. This has implications for our understanding of the roles of women within Roman farming and the Roman economy.

  10. Working at worship, worshipping at work: approaching material, practice, and value
    Matthew McCarty, K. A. Rask · pp. 239-255 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    This article analyzes the intersections between work and worship in the ancient Mediterranean from an archaeological perspective and provides an introduction to this special section. The goal is to challenge and move beyond two conceptual frames that have been deeply persistent in scholarship on the ancient world: (1) the imposition of two distinct category-containers “religion” and “work,” which we contend have been constructed and held separate due to particular idealist, capitalist, and colonialist ways of conceptualizing the past; (2) notions of the “embeddedness” of ancient religion, which we argue rarely acknowledge the underlying Polanyian economic baggage. We propose taking an explicitly relational and praxeological approach, drawing on notions of “lived religion” that, unlike the prevalent model of “Lived Ancient Religion,” often consider the intersections of production and worship, and focusing on the ways practices, spaces, materials, persons, and divine powers articulate in particular constellations.

  11. Chytra rituals in Athens
    Susan Rotroff · pp. 256-272 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    This essay examines the use of an ordinary household item – a chytra (stewpot) – to ritual effect. It focuses on Classical and Early Hellenistic deposits under the floors of a single room of the Classical Commercial Building, just north of the Athenian Agora. The plan of the building and workshop debris found in its floors identify it as a workplace, and the repeated appearance of the chytra and its orientation and relationship to the stratigraphy and other objects constitutes evidence of the ritual practices of those who worked there. Ancient texts and other chytra deposits, both in this building and elsewhere in the excavation, throw more light on these practices, constituting evidence for “religion-as-lived” in the Athenian workplace.

  12. A Vesta’d interest: apotropaia and shrines as vernacular risk management strategies in Vesuvian bakeries
    Abigail Joy Staub-Green · pp. 273-299 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    Pompeian commercial and industrial shrines are often cursorily lumped into the monolithic category of lararia , obfuscating their complexity. The shrines’ diverse combinations of deities, playful iconographies, and spatial placements suggest complex and dynamic strategies of appropriation and bricolage. Contextualized reassessment of assemblages and features reveals remnants of creative, “vernacular” ritual action. I argue that Pompeii’s pistrina proprietors prioritized familiar, locally invested deities alongside or even instead of the Lares to create a more personal, accessible space in their commercial ventures for reciprocal interaction with the divine – most notably, a variation of Vesta and her donkey. Spatial choices also indicate preferential construction of shrines in spaces that were particularly risky, with many closely associated with the mouth of the oven. But shrines were just one tool in a wider protective arsenal deployed around the volatile oven, which included amuletic and apotropaic interventions.

  13. A fishy business at Pompeii: the House of the Sarno Lararium
    David L. Stone · pp. 300-323 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    The House of the Sarno Lararium at Pompeii (I.14.7) is known for the enigmatic scene painted on the lararium in its rear courtyard. Following a common practice in classical archaeology of identifying an artistic scene based on a reference in an ancient text, it has been interpreted as documenting the arrival of onions. This article proposes a novel interpretation. I argue that the scene represents a group of fishermen bringing their catch back to port, weighing it, and preparing it for distribution. To support this interpretation, I show how the imagery on the lararium can be linked to fishing and the pictured deity, Sarnus. Employing theoretical concepts of “taskscapes” and “lived religion,” I argue that this scene of non-elite fishermen indicates how household worship was entangled with daily labor, as well as how daily labor took place against the backdrop of a ritual landscape.

  14. Wielding the axe, tending the priestly ornaments: religious labor in the emperor’s household
    Molly Swetnam-Burland · pp. 324-340 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    This paper investigates the ritual labor performed by the enslaved and freed members of the familia Caesaris who supported the emperor in his role as priest. Drawing on Roman art and funerary inscriptions, it contrasts elite and non-elite perspectives regarding religious work. Visual depictions – such as reliefs of the Ara Pacis and imperial sacrifice scenes – frame attendants as anonymous extensions of the emperor’s ritual authority, performing physical and sensory tasks that enhanced his prestige but denied their individuality. In contrast, inscriptions from the familia Caesaris record victimarii , musicians, and custodians of sacred implements who chose to highlight these roles in funerary commemoration. Their job-titles signaled skill, training, and membership in social networks that extended beyond imperial service, including participation in collegia and communal tombs. Examining these sources together reveals how ritual work was simultaneously devalued by elites and deeply meaningful to its practitioners, shaping identities, communities, and the lived experience of Roman religion.

    The title of this remarkable book may, in some respects, misrepresent the nature of its inquiry.It is not, in the strictest sense, an examination of how religion contributed to the making of Roman life.However, as McCarty astutely cautions, any attempt to uncover, much less fully grasp, the contours of Roman imperial hegemony in North Africa must begin with the critical unlearning of what he Fanon more searingly described as the pervasive, ambient effects of colonialism on the mental, emotional, and spatial environment of the colonized.3

  3. Writing as material culture in Early Italy
    Kathryn Lomas · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    The study of early writingThe purpose of writing, how it was transmitted from one culture to another, and its impact on society have been a highly contested field of study for many decades.The adoption of writing and the development of literacy had profound effects on ancient societies, but these are still imperfectly understood.This topic also poses many methodological problems, not least because structures of modern scholarship cut across the areas of study which are necessary to explore it fully.In this volume, Ruth Whitehouse (W.) aims to explore this from the standpoint of material culture.Although the title refers to writing throughout first millennium BC Italy, this volume is closely based on data gathered during two interconnected research projects which were limited to the development of writing in selected areas of Italy, 1 specifically northeast Italy, Puglia, the Lepontic areas of northwest Italy and the orientalising and archaic inscriptions from Etruria.In most previous studies of the subject, archaeologists, anthropologists, linguists and historians have all addressed the problem of the emergence of literacy and its implications for social and cultural development, but for the most part, they have worked in isolation from each other.The development of writing as a system, for instance, has tended to be studied in isolation from the social implications of literacy.2 There are also lively debates over the nature of writing between those who regard early writing, especially as preserved on durable materials, as essentially a visual and graphic medium and those who emphasise its role in storing and codifying information, 3 and on the interface between literacy and orality.4 A fundamental division persists between those who propose a structural model of writing, with an emphasis on transcription of sounds, rules of script development and transmission or see it as a primarily graphic form, the creation of systems of signs and symbols, and those who see it as a cultural practice embedded within specific societies, which can only be understood in terms of the needs, culture and practices of these societies.5

  4. Residential scaling, domestic architecture, and the housing stocks of Pompeii, Delos, Herculaneum, and Ostia
    J. W. Hanson, Marie Theres Wittmann · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-10-15
    摘要

    Although there has been considerable scholarly discussion of the relationships between the infrastructure and social and economic conditions of settlements and their estimated populations, there has been virtually no investigation of the extent to which it is possible to detect similar relationships within settlements, aside from some very preliminary work on Pompeii. The aim of this article is therefore to build on earlier work and investigate whether this is indeed the case by focusing on three other sites: Delos, Herculaneum, and Ostia. The results show that there are almost identical relationships between the numbers of rooms and the total ground floor footprints of the residential units within these settlements, despite their different social and economic contexts. This demonstrates, for the first time, that these effects are not just confined to one site and that we may be dealing with a relatively widespread phenomenon.

    摘要

    The volume Archeologia e archeometria delle miscele leganti di Aquileia romana e tardoantica (II sec.a.C.-VI sec.d.C.), published by Simone Dilaria (D.) in 2024 for the series Costruire nel mondo underlying unity of what has too often been studied in isolation.The originality of this research is evident on several closely interrelated levels.Its real strength certainly lies in considering
