  2. Earthen architecture and taskscapes in Neolithic northwestern Greece
    Dimitris Kloukinas · pp. 241-263 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    This paper investigates earthen architecture in Neolithic northwestern Greece through the study of fire-hardened daub, a frequently overlooked category of archaeological material. Drawing on the concept of ‘taskscape’, the present approach challenges static and linear perspectives of technical practice, emphasizing instead its material and temporal entanglements. By turning the focus on the spatiotemporal context of the construction process, including the immanent temporalities of building materials and techniques, and their integration with the agrarian economy, this analysis sheds light on the lived experience of Neolithic architectural practice. The synthesis of macroscopic and microscopic data, deriving primarily from a handful of systematically studied sites, offers regional insights into the synchronization and synchorization of building activities with environmental rhythms and the agrarian calendar. The study concludes that daub assemblages constitute high-resolution archives, materializing the rhythmic pulses and complex choreographies of Neolithic life.

  3. 5000 years of mudbrick at Arslantepe: ancient earthen architecture and modern sustainability
    Francesca Balossi Restelli, Elisa Biancifiori, Filippo Edoardo Capasso, Giuseppina Fazio, Giovanna Liberotti, Elisabetta Lulli et al. · pp. 264-283 · online 2026-02-26
    摘要

    Arslantepe is a 5000-year-old mound in eastern Anatolia featuring early examples of advanced planning, skilled craftsmanship and durable earthen architecture. Over the last four decades, it has been preserved through minimal, reversible interventions and protective shelters. Comparison with surviving traditional mudbrick houses in nearby Orduzu shows enduring continuities in design, materials, and maintenance. The loss of this know-how and its exclusion from modern building codes contrast with archaeological evidence of mudbrick’s structural potential. Drawing on local heritage, the authors propose integrating traditional methods with modern engineering to create sustainable, earthquake-resistant earthen architecture for reconstruction. This case highlights how cultural continuity and environmental sustainability can guide future rural housing policies.

  4. Building knowledge: anatomy of urban earthen architecture in the Pharaonic town of Elephantine
    Jessica Zuzanna Izak, Martin Sählhof · pp. 284-295 · online 2026-07-28
    摘要

    The incorporation of botanical additives into earthen building materials reflects not only practical construction techniques but also broader socioeconomic dynamics. At Elephantine, diachronic analysis of mudbricks, mortar, and plaster from Pharaonic Egypt reveals significant shifts in material recipes. These changes were shaped by evolving environmental conditions, agricultural strategies, and economic priorities, offering valuable insights into resource management practices in an urban context and the building knowledge of ancient workers. Tue selection and integration of plant temper demonstrate how construction methods aligned with the ecological and economic demands of ancient Egyptian society. This research highlights the interplay between material composition and resource use, providing a framework for understanding the adaptive strategies of past communities. Furthermore, the findings underscore the relevance of earthen architecture not only for sustainable building practices today but also for advancing the preservation of cultural heritage, emphasizing the enduring value of one of history’s most resilient architectural traditions.

  5. ‘New settlers in new fashion’: earthen architecture as a cultural marker in the Phoenician foundation of Eivissa (Ibiza, Spain)
    Benjamín Cutillas-Victoria, Marta Lorenzon, Marco Aurelio Esquembre, Bernia Sanz Kite, Joan Ramon Torres · pp. 296-315 · online 2026-04-20
    摘要

    Phoenician colonization brought significant cultural and environmental challenges for displaced groups across the Mediterranean Sea. Establishing new settlements required adapting Levantine practices to unfamiliar environments and raw materials. A paradigmatic example of such a community establishment occurred on the island of Ibiza (Balearic Islands, Spain), where a distinctive building tradition emerged, characterized by the use of mudbrick architecture. This paper presents a geoarchaeological study of earthen building materials from Eivissa, the principal site that flourished in the 7th century BC and has remained inhabited to the present day. Our methodology – combining archaeological analysis with geochemical and petrographic characterization – allows us to identify different raw material sources and construction recipes, reflecting how Phoenicians could have adapted to Ibiza’s specific environmental conditions. The results shed light on the development of the island’s mudbrick architecture and the probable challenges faced by Phoenician communities in preserving their traditional building techniques.

  6. Climate-responsive earthen architecture: multi-scale environmental analysis of passive house design in Classical Olynthos
    Matthew Fitzjohn, Andrew T. Wilson · pp. 316-335 · online 2026-05-29
    摘要

    This article presents the first quantitative analysis of the thermal performance of mud brick housing in Classical Greek cities. By integrating 3D architectural modelling with Rhinoceros 3D and the Ladybug and Honeybee environmental simulation toolsets, it introduces a novel methodological framework for studying ancient earthen architecture. Focusing on housing blocks at Olynthos, the study examines how architectural form, material properties, and house location shaped indoor climates. Results demonstrate that mud brick moderated temperature fluctuations, yet specific building forms and locations created perceptible differences in thermal comfort between rooms, storeys and house position within a city block. By moving beyond purely morphological or economic readings of domestic space, this research shows how earthen architecture shaped lived social experience and advances debates on the temporality of life, equality and standards of living in the Classical polis.

  7. Earthen dialogues: the cosmopolitics of adobe architectural construction in the Jequetepeque Valley of Peru (600–1000 CE)
    Rachel Schloss · pp. 336-355 · online 2026-06-29
    摘要

    At the scale of the individual brick, adobe construction preserves a record of the decisions, labour, and political relationships through which it was made. This study compares Huaca Colorada and Tecapa, two neighbouring ceremonial centres in the Jequetepeque Valley, whose construction practices diverged markedly during the late Middle Horizon (600–1000 CE). Through morphological, grain-size, and pXRF analyses of over 1,600 bricks, distinct construction regimes emerge at each site: heterogeneous, inclusion-rich bricks and episodic building at Huaca Colorada reflect a decentralized process tied to ritual cycles and community labour, while standardized brick types and coordinated planning at Tecapa suggest a hierarchically organized regime consistent with highland building traditions. A discrete morphological and material convergence in Huaca Colorada’s final phase, concentrated in the east-facing platform oriented towards Tecapa, is interpreted as evidence of inter-site political negotiation, registered in the dimensions, sediment makeup, and geochemistry of individual bricks.

  8. Tomb living: shaped-earth furniture in re-used tombs of Dra Abu el-Naga, Luxor
    Ana Oliveira, Salima Ikram · pp. 356-377 · online 2026-03-26
    摘要

    For much of the 19th and 20th centuries, Egypt’s Theban necropolis (Luxor) was a living palimpsest where ancient tombs mingled with modern mudbrick homes. The Qurnawis, the modern inhabitants of the necropolis, adapted pharaonic tombs, using earthen architecture to convert them into functional living spaces. Shaped-earth cupboards, shelves, troughs, ovens, and dovecotes were constructed both inside and outside the tomb-houses. This paper presents unpublished data from the Spanish archaeological mission at Dra Abu el-Naga North (Djehuty Project), documenting the surviving examples of modern earthen architecture within the Middle and New Kingdom tombs (2030–1070 BCE). The modern earthen architecture is possibly a continuation of an ancient tradition of the inhabitants of the necropolis, and of Egypt. Today, these earthen structures are nearly extinct, having been demolished, or replaced. Understanding and protecting these features offers invaluable insights into the daily life of Egypt’s modern inhabitants as well as their ancient counterparts.

  9. Building one’s own prison: mudbrick production and embodied prisoner labour in Kazakhstan’s Soviet-era Gulags
    Katherine M. Erdman · pp. 378-393 · online 2026-08-03
    摘要

    One of the Soviet Gulag’s largest camp systems was Karlag, located in central Kazakhstan. The project, Central Asia’s Gulags, is the first to explore this expansive forced labour system from an archaeological perspective. Recent satellite and pedestrian surveys have identified annular pit features common to multiple camps. This study presents preliminary survey results from the site of Aktailak, a camp point within Karlag, with a focus on camp areas dense with annular pits. Data gathered from the targeted excavations of two barracks are used to calculate and humanize prisoner labour involved in mudbrick construction by focusing on embodied practices. Additionally, as unique features, annular pits have the potential to help identify unknown prison camps in the vast Kazakh Steppe.

Online first（尚未編入期別）· 30
  1. Replica revolutions in rock art: from physical replicas to virtual reality libraries via contemporary art
    David Robinson, Nathan Jones · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-10-30
    摘要

    Rock-art replicas generally are judged as more or less faithful substitutes for inaccessible originals. While previous studies have demonstrated the value of replicas for individual archaeological experiments, we reconceive them as persistent interdisciplinary research infrastructure. Drawing on reflections from original sites, we compare physical copies with virtual environments, developing criteria for evaluating replicas. Physical replicas preserve material, spatial and atmospheric relationships but constrain enquiry through fixed routes and authoritative narratives; VR replicas increase freedom, manipulation and perceptual experimentation but sacrifice embodied materiality. Neither currently supports cumulative research: observations disappear when the encounter ends. Central to this contribution is a two-directional account of indexicality: replicas point backwards to the caves and their makers, but also outwards to the scanners, artists, software, institutions and interpretive decisions that produce them. We therefore propose the perma-library as an infrastructure through which replicas can become comparable, revisitable, annotatable and capable of accumulating research over time.

  2. Early large-scale petroglyphs in Central Scandinavia – the relevance of landscape and hunting contexts for implicit narrativity
    Tomas Persson, Peter Skoglund, Michael Ranta, Jan Magne Gjerde, Anna Cabak Rédei · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-10-30
    摘要

    Some of the earliest Scandinavian petroglyphs depict big game, such as elks and reindeer, dating from around 9000 to around 4000 BCE. These large images were represented as singular motifs, usually without any interaction with other figures, and without any explicit narrative appearance. However, involvement with the surrounding natural landscape and hunting contexts, particularly river rapids and straits, seems to have affected the placement of the images and likely given them narrative implications. This involvement may have given the earliest large-scale rock petroglyphs an implicit narrative functionality. Specifically, due to their size and visibility, especially from waterways, the images could have had an effective indexical function as ‘signposts’ at strategic hunting grounds related to water crossings. In order to corroborate these assumptions, examples from Sweden (Landverk, Gärde, and Duved) and Norway (Stykket) will be discussed, and contrasted with a later miniaturizing tradition.

  3. Edible insects as heritage foods: towards an archaeology of alternative proteins for food sustainability
    Lisa-Marie Shillito · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    Insects and their secondary products form a persistent component of human diets from the Palaeolithic onwards, and entomophagy remains a common practice in many parts of the world. Coprolite analysis, biomarker and volatile organic compound (VOC) screening, ceramic and lithic residues, contextual caches, and isotopes provide direct and indirect evidence for entomophagy across diverse settings, supported by ethnographic and textual sources, but are rarely considered in wider archaeological narratives of subsistence. This article makes two arguments. First, the marginal place of insects in many archaeological syntheses reflects taphonomic filters, methodological priorities, and interpretive traditions rather than absence in the past. Second, archaeology is uniquely positioned to reframe insects as heritage foods in contemporary sustainability debates. By demonstrating that Western disgust is historically contingent and translating archaeological findings into accessible encounters, archaeology can help destigmatize entomophagy and support a shift to more diverse, resilient protein systems aligned with sustainable development goals.

  4. Fire as spatial organizer: reconstructing the ritual grammar of a late Longshan ceremonial landscape at Yuhuicun, China
    Rui Bai · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-10-19
    摘要

    This paper investigates the spatial organization of fire-related deposits at Yuhuicun, a late Longshan period ceremonial complex in the Huai River Valley, China. Archaeological research has explored the role of fire in human adaptation, but less attention has been paid to how ritual fire was organized in space. Yuhuicun’s burnt sacrificial surface (BSS, ca. 90 m2) lies at the centre of a purpose-built platform surrounded by sacrificial pits, a trench containing nearly 18,000 sherds and diverse activity features. Drawing on theories of ritual materialization, structured deposition and elemental ontology, this study constructs a 126-feature spatial database and applies four complementary approaches: spatial association analysis, compositional patterning, functional clustering and co-occurrence network analysis. The results suggest that the fire at Yuhuicun played a central role in the site’s spatial organization, with the BSS as the focus of a ritual landscape consisting of two main areas linked by built features. The BSS preserves intense burning but no accumulated ash, while the adjacent trench is saturated with combustion residues, indicating deliberate redistribution.

  5. From mountain flames to domestic smoke: rescaling ritual fire in the Gubbio valley from the Bronze Age to Imperial Rome
    Marianna Negro · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    Archaeological accounts of religion in the Gubbio valley have often presented incorporation into the Roman world as a rupture, privileging public expressions of Roman religion. This leaves unresolved how locally embedded ritual practices changed across this transition. This paper investigates the changing scale and location of fire-related ritual from the Final Bronze Age to the Roman Imperial period. It combines contextual analysis of excavated evidence from Monte Ingino and Monte Ansciano with typological and GIS-based spatial analysis of lamps and incense burners recorded by the Gubbio Project. Burnt deposits and faunal assemblages indicate communal practices involving fire and feasting at elevated locations during the Final Bronze and Early Iron Ages. Sixth-century BCE construction and votive deposition at Monte Ansciano marked more explicitly defined collective ritual activity. Roman-period lamps and second-century CE incense burners reveal the dispersal of fire, light and smoke into household and rural settings, demonstrating the repeated rescaling and reformulation of ritual practice across changing contexts.

  6. Carrying fire: containing sacred and profane flame in the Aegean Bronze Age
    Carl Knappett · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-09-10
    摘要

    In this article, I explore the uses of fire in the Aegean Bronze Age, and specifically Minoan Crete. Despite a rich archaeological record with plentiful traces of burning, fire has received much less attention than might be expected. Particularly curious is the relative occlusion of fire in scholarship on Minoan buildings. I therefore propose a methodology that seeks to situate fire in relation to both its specific technicities and its architectural milieux. One important distinction is between fixed and portable hearths and their different functions. While the fixed hearth is in later periods often associated with the sacred, this connection is hard to make in the Minoan context. However, portable equipment for fire holds out more promise with both sacred and profane uses likely. The article further reflects on the implications of fire’s portability for our understanding of Minoan conceptions of interior space, architectural aesthetics and atmosphere.

  7. Bridging morphometrics and Archaeogenetics: a non-destructive approach to species identification of sheep, goats and pigs based on bony labyrinth
    Agathe Guignet, Adeline Hemelsdaël, Daniel Gaspar, Áine Halpin, Valeria Mattiangeli, Vanessa Navarrete et al. · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-09-07
    摘要

    The petrous part of the temporal bone is a target for ancient DNA research due to its biomolecular preservation, yet destructive sampling risks losing valuable morphological information. Bony labyrinth morphology, captured by high-resolution 3D imaging, carries strong taxonomic and evolutionary signals, but its bioarchaeological application remains limited. We assessed the potential of bony labyrinth geometric morphometrics (GMM) for species identification of domestic animals by analysing 14 micro-CT-scanned archaeological petrosals from Spain alongside a dataset of 41 modern French and Spanish breeds of sheep and goats, as well as two pigs and two wild boars. Predictive discriminant analysis shows bony labyrinth shape reliably distinguishes these taxa, matching ancient genomic identification. Our findings demonstrate that bony labyrinth morphometrics is a robust, non-destructive approach for taxonomic identification of archaeological specimens, complementing other bioarchaeological proxies (e.g. ancient DNA, palaeoprotein). Integrated with palaeogenomic evidence, this framework enables comprehensive reconstructions of domestication processes and human-animal relationships.

  8. Revealing archaeological olive diversity: insights from stone geometric morphometrics, experimental charring, and varietal identification (Port-la-Nautique, France, Roman period)
    Juliette Testas, Allowen Evin, Sarah Ivorra, Margaux Tillier, Corinne Sanchez, Nicolas Carayon et al. · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-09-07
    摘要

    The cultivated olive tree, domesticated in the Levant by the mid-5th millennium BCE, spread westward across the Mediterranean to reach southern France by 600 BCE. Although geometric morphometrics applied to archaeological olive stones has provided insights into this diffusion, morphotype identification remains challenging, and the effects of charring, the most common preservation mode, are poorly understood. We compared ten morphometric approaches to determine the most effective method for discriminating wild and domesticated morphologies and distinguishing varieties. Controlled charring experiments assessed the effects of temperature, duration, and atmosphere on stone morphometry. The resulting methodology was applied to 261 olive stones from the Roman villa of Port-la-Nautique and the Capelles fishpond (Narbonne, France). Elliptic Fourier Transforms proved most effective for taxonomic discrimination, and charring up to 350°C did not prevent reliable classification against modern uncharred references. This approach enabled the identification, for the first time, of archaeological stones resembling present-day varieties, offering new insights into the evolution of olive agrobiodiversity.

  9. Graded illumination as institutional practice: multi-nozzled lamps in public contexts across the Roman Mediterranean
    Ofer Gat · pp. 1-26 · online 2026-08-17
    摘要

    This article examines multi-nozzled lamps recovered exclusively from public contexts across the Roman Mediterranean to investigate illumination as an institutional practice. Moving beyond typological description, it analyzes how variations in nozzle count and spatial deployment produce graded regimes of visibility within civic architecture. Drawing on a systematically assembled comparative dataset, the study identifies structured regional patterns in the distribution of enhanced lighting across agoras, bathhouses, palatial complexes, streets, and administrative buildings. The findings demonstrate that intensified illumination was neither randomly dispersed nor merely functional. Rather, lighting capacity was selectively embedded within architectural systems to organize attention, regulate movement, and reinforce hierarchies of presence. By integrating theories of space, visibility, infrastructure, and sensory experience, the article proposes that multi-nozzled lamps function as micro-infrastructural devices mediating institutional authority. Illumination is thus interpreted as a form of graded social materiality through which public consciousness and civic order were materially produced across the Roman Mediterranean.

  10. Creation by fire: ritual burning and ancestor shrines in West Africa
    Stephen A. Dueppen, Daphne Gallagher · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-06
    摘要

    Fire-hardened walls, burned floors and completely burned structures are common features of archaeological sites in central West Africa, often forming distinct strata in sites that developed into large mounds, or tells. Despite their ubiquity, the presence of burning on architectural remnants plays a very minor role in archaeological interpretations. We suggest that burned features are frequently the result of different forms of sacrificial burning that created durable ancestor shrines to anchor the past, living and future community. We contextualize ritualized burning within a broader cultural setting where fire is transformative and creative, with extensive religious dimensions that are both central to the important historic social status of blacksmiths and potters and fundamental to sacrificial practices more broadly. We argue that architectural burning is part of this broader corpus of transmutation by fire involving both divine power and extensions of the human soul that created material attachments critical to ancestral presence.

  11. House-burning as a form of prehistoric potlatching
    John Chapman, Bisserka Gaydarska · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-08-03
    摘要

    The potlatching practices of the First Nations of the Northwest Pacific Coast have been used as ethnographic analogies for Neolithic, Bronze Age and historic ceremonies. Its relevance in prehistory was aided by the often dramatic materialization of the potlatch’s accompanying ceremonies. What does potlatching have to do with house-burning in the European Neolithic? The experimental house-building and -burning programme in the Nebelivka Project, Kirovograd Oblast, Ukraine (2009–2017) confirmed earlier suggestions that much additional fuel over and above that contained in the original structure was required to achieve successful conflagration, supporting the explanation of deliberate burning. But this left a second problem – why should communities sacrifice such apparently solid, well-built and recently constructed structures? Here, the analogy with potlatching becomes more apparent, not only with the destruction of valuable objects but also the linkage of potlatches to the building or repair of a house in some Northwest Coast societies. We propose to use an informal Venn diagram approach to study these potential linkages. The results suggest that there is sufficient convergence between deliberate house-burning and potlatching to further our understanding of the former through insights from the latter.

  12. The evolution of fire and ritual: a Durkheimian perspective
    Michael Chazan · pp. 1-11 · online 2026-07-27
    摘要

    Durkheim envisioned the evolution of religion as an expression of the dialectical relationship between people living in society with the forces that emerge from this society. His evolutionary scenario was built on living and historic societies, primarily Aboriginal Australians, Biblical Jews, Catholics, and 19th Century cosmopolitan Europeans. This article explores the potential of applying Durkheim’s conceptual framework to the palaeoanthropological record with a focus on the potential role of fire in the emergence of ritual. This inquiry entails a consideration of the structure of evolutionary processes and the current archaeological record for the early engagement with fire.

  13. Carved in stone and written as script. Material expressions of Bektashi identity in 19th c. Epirus (NW Greece)
    Faidon Moudopoulos-Athanasiou · pp. 1-12 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    Ottoman-era archaeology is gradually emerging in dialogue with art history and Ottoman studies. Yet aspects of material culture traditionally addressed by our discipline remain understudied and disjointed. This paper investigates stone carvings and Ottoman inscriptions from 19th-century Epirus, focusing on the town of Konitsa (NW Greece). We propose a synthetic approach that treats stone carvings and inscriptions as a distinct but complementary means of manifesting identity. Through a microhistorical comparative analysis of two neighbouring architectural complexes belonging to Bektashi-affiliated households, we use these media to illuminate the identities of local powerholders during an era of political upheaval and imperial reform. The contrast between vernacular, locally produced carvings and standardized, imported inscriptions reflects differing political orientations and strategies of cultural resilience in response to transformations within the Ottoman Empire.

  14. Turn the light on: features related to the Roman lamps’ lighting
    Carlos Pereira · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-07-07
    摘要

    The relationship between lamps and fire has too often been taken for granted rather than critically examined. Research has largely focused on the morphology, iconography, epigraphy, production and trade of lamps, while paying less attention to their function and their relationship with fire. Yet classical literature provides valuable evidence for the association of lamp flames with both gods and humans in Antiquity, offering insights that the archaeological record alone rarely reveals. Understanding how people used, perceived and ritualised artificial light requires a multisource approach that integrates textual, archaeological and iconographic evidence. Artificial light was far more than a practical necessity. In the Roman world, fire permeated nearly every domain of life, serving both everyday activities and religious practice. Lamp flames embodied a striking duality: they could sustain life or cause destruction, illuminate domestic spaces or accompany the divine, acting as both witnesses and agents of human experience. Their light was simultaneously functional, symbolic and protective. Recognising this complexity broadens our understanding of lamps beyond their material form, reminding us that these artefacts cannot be separated from the flames they once held. When we study lamps, we are also studying the light that illuminated the gods and people in Antiquity.

  15. Multidisciplinary analyses of Gotlandic picture stones: a case study of fragments from Martebo and Stenkyrka
    Hannah Strehlau, Patrick Hänsel, Laila Kitzler Åhfeldt, Magnus Källström, Michael Fergusson, Sigmund Oehrl · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    The Gotlandic picture stones are an unparalleled monumental art in Scandinavia whose rich imagery offers a glimpse into pre-Christian belief systems. Their fragmented condition and reuse in secondary contexts, however, has challenged our understanding of their original use. Against this background, this article matches the two Migration Period picture stones GP 268 Martebo kyrka I and GP 428 Stenkyrka kyrka 46 which were previously considered separate monuments. Because the fragments lack matching fracture edges and display apparent inconsistencies in their decoration, we conducted a multidisciplinary investigation using photogrammetry, groove analysis, runology, and isotope analysis. The results demonstrate that the stones share the same width, possess complementing decorative elements, and originate from the same geological rock layer on Gotland. Groove analysis further indicates that the majority of the decoration expresses the same carving characteristics. The runic inscription was likely added to GP 268 Martebo kyrka I during the Migration Period as part of the original decoration. Taken together, these findings support the affiliation of GP 268 Martebo kyrka I and GP 428 Stenkyrka kyrka 46, identifying them as a single monument and a key monument of phase A Gotlandic picture stones (400–550 CE).

  16. The Northern Tradition rock art of western Norway: from archaeological relativism to iconographic relevance under Late Mesolithic demographic change
    Trond Lødøen · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-07-03
    摘要

    The paper argues that more holistic and theoretically informed approaches to contextualizing the Northern Tradition rock art of Scandinavia have significantly expanded the interpretive potential of its iconography. By integrating archaeological excavations, independent scientific analyses, and extensive radiocarbon dating procedures, a more robust chronological framework has emerged, situating the production of Northern tradition rock art firmly to the end of the Late Mesolithic. This chronological anchoring has enabled a reconceptualization of the imagery not merely as isolated symbolic expressions, but as practices embedded within contemporaneous material, social, and perhaps even ecological transformations. The demonstrated synchrony between iconographic themes and demographic shifts at the term of the Late Mesolithic further suggests that the imagery may be read as an active engagement with – and representation of – structures and features once part of prehistoric dynamics. The paper also critiques prevailing interpretive models shaped by modern Western utilitarian and consumption-based assumptions, arguing that such frameworks still constrain our ability to access alternative ontological premises. Instead, it advocates theoretical approaches inspired by multispecies perspectives, relational ontologies, and broader anthropological debates on animism and posthumanism, to reconstruct past premises. From this standpoint, the Northern Tradition iconography may be understood as preserving traces of Late Mesolithic worldviews in which humans, animals, landscapes, and non-human entities were entangled within shared social and cosmological fields. This perspective not only opens new interpretive pathways for understanding the epistemic content encoded in the imagery and tentatively helps to explain the sudden appearance of rock art, but also provides a more expansive foundation for revisiting and recontextualising the wider corpus of Northern Tradition rock art.

  17. Joining hands: cross-cultural analysis of decorated handprints
    Barbara Oosterwijk, Linda Hurcombe, Jamie Hampson, José Iriarte · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    Hand depictions are among the most widespread motifs in global rock art, appearing from the Pleistocene to the present and often representing some of the earliest symbolic practices. At Cerro Azul and Paredones del Potrero in Amazonian Colombia, red handprints feature diverse decorations – including spirals, braided lines and geometric infill – whose production and significance remain little understood. This study presents the first detailed typological classification of thirteen decorated handprint types from these sites. Quantitative and spatial analyses are integrated with ethnographic and Indigenous perspectives, together with cross-cultural comparisons from regions including Brazil, Borneo and Australia, to explore how decorated hands may have functioned as embodied gestures, markers of identity and elements of cosmological landscapes. By combining fine-grained local documentation with a global comparative framework, the study demonstrates the potential of decorated handprints to illuminate the interplay of body, material and place in the creation of enduring social and symbolic relationships.

  18. Carved stone in Mesolithic Cornwall: portable art, Mesolithic magic or mundane markings. Extraordinary new discoveries from southwest Britain
    Chris Carey, Andy M. Jones, Anna Lawson-Jones, Jo May, Laura Ratcliffe-Warren, Sean Taylor · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-06-12
    摘要

    Shaped and carved stone is a fundamental component of the archaeological record from the Mesolithic period across the UK. Whilst flint is the central element of this archaeological record, there is also a significant non-flint lithic component to many British Mesolithic sites that has been somewhat understudied. Within Cornwall, south-west Britain, research into this non-flint lithic component, partially retrieved through innovative methods of whole ‘earth’ sampling of Mesolithic land-surfaces, has revealed a significant collection of Mesolithic carved stones. A selection of these stones is presented and consideration is given to whether these pieces were created from their use as tools or whether these pieces of stone have longer biographies, potentially eventually becoming mnemonic devices. Parallels are drawn to other Mesolithic carvings from across the UK, with some symbols such as the ‘X’s and parallel lines, evident over a wide area and across the long time period of the Mesolithic.This paper is dedicated to our dear friend and colleague Andy Jones.

  19. Stone workshops between petrography and iconography: a preliminary study on Iron Age basalt carvings
    Cristiana Biancalani, Marina Pucci, Chiara Colombo, Maria Catrambone, Antonio Sansonetti · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-06-09
    摘要

    The flourishing of sculptural series on orthostats, protomes and statues, which decorated the so-called Syro-Hittite city-state citadels, represents a unique phenomenon in stone production during the pre-classical period (12th-7th centuries BC) in the Syro-Anatolian region. Since the first discoveries of these sculptural cycles, archaeological and art-historical research has primarily focused on problems related to style, dating and production, mostly through an iconographic approach. However, the matter of the workshops (from quarrymen to sculptors) has not been fully explored, nor has the integration of geological aspects (such as the identification of quarries) and the production process. This article presents preliminary results from a broader project that addresses this issue through iconographic and petrographic analysis of basalt samples from sculptures found at Syro-Hittites sites. This study aims to introduce an interdisciplinary approach, seeking to bridge the existing gap between workshops, quarries and the Syro-Hittites political entities that commissioned these monumental artworks.

  20. The persistence of memory: reuse and resignification of prehistoric petroglyphs in historical times. Two case studies from Malagón (La Mancha, Spain)
    Álvaro Ibero, Luis Benítez de Lugo Enrich, Álvaro Guzmán Sil · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-06-05
    摘要

    This paper presents two unpublished petroglyph sites from Malagón (La Mancha, inner Iberia) as case studies in the long-term cultural reuse and reinterpretation of prehistoric rock art. Through a diachronic-biographical approach that combines archaeological, historical, and ethnographic sources, our research has identified several graphic phases spanning from Late Prehistory to the 19th century. These include cup-marks, schematic motifs, inscriptions, Christian iconography, and modern imagery. Moreover, interviews with locals indicate that these sites have remained in use through to the present day. Consequently, both sites represent an ideal scenario to explore how different human communities have persistently engaged with these sites throughout millennia, attributing new meanings aligned with changing worldviews. Thus, rather than conceiving archaeological sites as static relics, the study emphasizes their dynamic role in the construction of collective memory and identity. Ultimately, all this supports the notion that integrating traditional knowledge into archaeological interpretation can result in a more inclusive and socially grounded approach to heritage preservation.

  21. Beyond context: a productive approach to Iberian sculpture
    Arturo García-López · pp. 1-11 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    Iberian sculpture poses major interpretive challenges due to the frequent loss of primary archaeological contexts. Traditionally approached through typological and stylistic frameworks, these works are often recovered in secondary settings or have been reused, making their original functions difficult to determine. This article adopts a biographical and productive perspective, tracing Iberian carved stone from quarrying and carving to reuse and final deposition. By focusing on material processes and labour, rather than solely on form or symbolism, it reframes sculpture as a product embedded in social and economic relations. The paper also reflects on the interpretive consequences of context loss and transformation, contributing to broader discussions on the biography of carved stone and the challenges of reconstructing meaning from displaced materials.

  22. Stone panopticum: rethinking on the monumental stone sculpture of the Iron Age Histri
    Martina Blečić Kavur · pp. 1-12 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    Stone as a durable and symbolic material played a central role in the visual culture and social practices of Histrian communities during the last millennium BCE. This paper examines the fragmented stone sculpture from Nesactium (Croatia), focussing on its meaning, function and cultural context. Using the concepts of material agency and object biography, it shows how these monuments, in addition to their monumentality, structured space and belonging, shaped collective memory and social identities as well as ideological forms of expression and representation. Nesactium sculpture thus emerges as a visually articulated social practice with a multi-layered and multifunctional role within the community. Their production testifies to a high level of artisanship and strong links to the Italo-Adriatic cultural sphere.

  23. Lapidarian liturgies: mark making as ritual practice and technological process in Graeco-Roman magical gems
    Alice Clinch · pp. 1-13 · online 2026-06-01
    摘要

    ‘Magical gems’ form a specific corpus of the larger category of glyptic art from Graeco-Roman antiquity. Decorated in rich iconography and text, they are important markers of more personal forms of religious practice. This paper argues that it is not only their iconography or text which bestows them with their magical properties but also the process by which they are crafted. Through exploring methods of production and the relationship between carving, engraving, and mark making, this paper explores the thinking, cognition, and ritual action that may have been involved. The paper concludes that through a process of mark making, gems became actively charged agents, which embodied religious and magical properties. These, in turn, were mediated through visual and haptic encounters which were central to their use and ability to function.

  24. Flames of creation: rituals of fire, sacrifice, and renewal in the ancient Andes
    Edward Swenson, Ellen Pacheco, John P. Warner · pp. 1-31 · online 2026-05-19
    摘要

    The distinctive materiality (qualia) of fire can account for its near universal sacred status, including in past Andean societies. As a brilliant manifestation of destructive-creation, fire epitomizes a highly-motivated, non-arbitrary sign, thus explaining its intimate link with sacrifice, beginnings, endings, life, and empowering transformation. In the context of a select survey of global traditions, we mobilize a case study from the Moche temple of Huaca Colorada (700–900 CE) in the Jequetepeque Valley of northern Peru to demonstrate that fire served as an important medium in rituals of sacrificial consumption essential for metabolic creation and growth, and by extension, social and cosmic reproduction. The immolation of ceremonial constructions in rededication rites and the sacrifice and interment of individuals associated with intense burning events at the temple support this argument. We further contend that fire-based rites complemented pyro-technologies widely practiced at Huaca Colorada, and cooking and craft industries formed part of integrated religious liturgies. Ultimately, a comparison of Huaca Colorada with the neighbouring ceremonial centre of Huaca Dos Cruces (1000–1350 CE) reveals that despite fire’s inherent material-semiotic properties, its meaning and ritual efficacy varied historically and appears to have changed in the post-Moche period in the Jequetepeque Valley.

  25. Flat white or with a round character? New light on stone funerary slabs and orbs from south Sweden
    Ing-Marie Back Danielsson · pp. 1-12 · online 2026-04-09
    摘要

    Stone funerary orbs with carved ornamentation, along with similarly decorated stones, but in the form of slabs, are a distinctive yet underexplored category of archaeological objects from Late Iron Age Sweden. These carved stones are originally found in burial contexts from the Migration Period (c. 400–550 CE) and at times early Vendel Period (c. 550–800 CE). In later centuries, they are known to have been removed from their original context, and re-used for other purposes. This paper discusses stone funerary orbs and slabs from the Mälar Valley, south Sweden, and sheds light on their different object itineraries throughout time. In doing so, it focusses on the making of their ornamentation, and its (desired) affects and effects, as well as the possible qualities that were associated with the stone itself (e.g. form, colour, descent) and how these aspects may have played pivotal roles in their removal from burial contexts to new ontological realms.

  26. Shape analyses and the human skeleton
    Kimberly A. Plomp · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-03-26
    摘要

    One key theme running through human skeletal research from early studies in the 18th century to today is a focus on shape. Unfortunately, early research fell prey to racist ideas about human biology. But thankfully, over the last 100 years, there has been a refocus on how to interpret the shape of human bones and what we can learn from it. We now answer a variety of questions using shape, ranging from how and why certain anatomical traits evolved to constructing a biological profile of an individual. Advancements in the methods used to capture and analyse shape also enable archiving and sharing of large amounts of digital data. This paper reviews how shape has been used to study the human skeleton, outline advancements, and reflect on trajectories for future research. It also presents ethical considerations of shape analyses and open science within human skeletal research from a global perspective.

  27. Getting into shape: computing continua of Middle Stone Age prepared core technology
    Antoine Muller, Chris Clarkson · pp. 1-26 · online 2026-03-20
    摘要

    Levallois technology is a particularly skilful and cognitively intensive lithic technology where preparatory core shaping is aimed at producing predetermined flake blanks. It has significant implications for hominin evolution and dispersals, yet remains notoriously difficult to define relative to other prepared cores. Significant variability in prepared core techno-morphology is often rendered into a handful of rarely agreed upon discrete categories. We ask whether existing classifications of cores are immutable, discrete groups, or whether there are unbroken continua of prepared core variability. With a bespoke set of 3D computational methods, we directly quantify the criteria used to define Levallois technology. Applied to a diverse sample of prepared cores from five African MSA sites, these methods establish continua of prepared core preparation, recurrence and exhaustion. Quantifying this continuous variability in how blanks are removed from cores can help holistically address past hominin technological decision making, investment and flexibility.

  28. On the historical mode of existence of rock art: making, chaîne opératoire, and history
    Andrés Troncoso · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-03-13
    摘要

    This paper discusses a theoretical and methodological approach to exploring the historical dimension of rock art from a post-representational perspective. We propose that by making and experiencing rock art, a field of historical relations among humans, non-humans, materials, and places was woven, allowing the emergence of the affective properties of rock art and its historical mode of existence. This field of relations can be traced by reconstructing the chaîne opératoire of rock art, considering the intertwining of four sequences (images, instruments, pigmentary material, performativity of bodies) and four analytical dimensions (material, visual, spatial, temporal). Finally, these ideas are briefly discussed by comparing the rock art of hunter-gatherers and agrarian groups in central-north Chile

  29. The punctuated itineraries of purple porphyry
    Linda R. Gosner, Leena Ghannam · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-03-06
    摘要

    Porphyry is a reddish-purple, white-speckled stone that was quarried in Egypt’s Eastern Desert under Roman rule. This distinctive stone has been frequently re-carved, reused, and imitated from antiquity to the present. The Tracing Purple Porphyry Project (TPPP) traces temporal, spatial, and contextual patterns in porphyry reuse to understand the stone’s changing meaning and value. Drawing on data from the TPPP, this article employs the concept of ‘punctuated itineraries’ – rooted in scholarship on object biographies – to understand how episodes of physical transformation have reignited earlier meanings of porphyry for new audiences. We introduce two notable kinds of punctuations, embellishment and miniaturization. These are exemplified here by a Roman amphora adorned with the appendages of an eagle in the medieval period and a miniature porphyry obelisk carved in eighteenth-century Rome. Ultimately, they demonstrate how punctuations can activate the stone’s enduring associations with Rome’s imperial and Christian legacies and its exoticizing Egyptian origins.

  30. Revisiting Creswell Crags: applying digital methods to reassess the northernmost examples of Upper Palaeolithic cave art
    Izzy Wisher, Lisa-Elen Meyering, Barbara Oosterwijk · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-03-02
    摘要

    The engraved parietal art of Creswell Crags, discovered in 2003, remains the only example of Palaeolithic cave art in the U.K. and represents the northernmost signature of Upper Palaeolithic artistic behaviour. Despite its unique nature and significant advances in digital techniques used to evaluate parietal art, it has received limited academic attention in the past two decades since its discovery. We utilized a suite of digital modelling techniques (high-resolution photography, close-range photogrammetry, Reflectance Transformation Imaging) to reassess the art. Our results demonstrate the potential of such techniques to revise the original interpretations of the art and provide tentative, yet accessible, evidence of previously unidentified engravings. Our work also produced an accessible digital repository with vast potential for future research into this important site.
